ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U1	Let us move on to agenda item 15, which is the first reading of the Bill 753 on Amendments to the Tobacco Act and related laws initiated by the Government of the Republic. I ask Urmas Kruuse, Minister of Health and Labour of the Riigikogu Chair!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U2	The honourable President of the sitting! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! I am pleased to present to you the draft amendment to the Tobacco Act and related laws which the Riigikogu adopted on 21 October. This is a draft aimed at ensuring the protection of the health of people, in particular minors, from the harmful effects of tobacco products. The draft law introduces a ban on the sale to minors of products intended for the use of tobacco products, products and their components similar to tobacco products, and a ban on minors from using, acquiring, possessing and possessing tobacco products similar to tobacco products. We would like minors not to consume products similar to those used in tobacco products and products intended for tobacco products not to be available to minors. The sale of tobacco products to young people and consumption have so far been completely unregulated. Although the current Tobacco Act provides for a ban on the marketing of tobacco products in the form of tobacco products to minors, this has unfortunately proved insufficient. The draft therefore also introduces a ban on the use of products similar to tobacco products in the premises of children's institutions, pre-school children's institutions, primary schools, upper secondary schools, vocational schools, hobbies, open youth centres or youth permanent camps and youth project camps, and in their restricted areas. The aim of the amendment is to ensure the protection of human health, in particular the protection of minors, as the use of products similar to those used in tobacco products (including nicotine-free) normalises smoking as a socially accepted standard of behaviour for minors and creates a favourable environment for tobacco products to be used. E-cigarettes, as the most common tobacco product, emits carcinogenic substances in space and the propylene glycol used as solvent causes irritation of the eyes and airways. Nicotine is also released into the air. Although the quantities of harmful substances in it are lower than cigarette smoke, there is reason to prohibit the use of the product by the abovementioned authorities. The draft bans minors from staying in smoking rooms and smoking areas in order to protect their health. In view of the immediate risk to children's health, restrictions are necessary. The WHO Framework Convention on Tobacco Control calls on the Contracting Parties to the Convention to implement all requirements of the Convention on smoke-free tobacco for the protection of the health of their citizens, pending the ban on the import, production and sale of products. In view of the risks posed by tobacco products for oral use, the widespread misunderstanding about the safety of the product and the popularity of minors, it is in the interest of public health to prohibit the handling of chewing tobacco in addition to other smoke-free tobacco products, i.e. to prohibit the handling of all smoke-free tobacco products. The bill establishes the quantities of smoking-free tobacco products prohibited for their handling, which may be delivered to Estonia by a natural person for non-commercial purposes, i.e. for personal use with him in mobile luggage, as well as the large quantity of smoke-free tobacco products prohibited for handling, and the prohibition on transporting smoke-free tobacco products to Estonia by mail or other means. The draft implements four measures of the Green Paper on tobacco policy. Firstly, amendments to the tobacco law. Prohibition of the handling of smoke-free tobacco products. From 1 January 2016, the Tobacco Act introduces a large volume of tobacco-free tobacco products not allowed to be handled in 300 packages. It shall be prohibited for a minor to sell or offer for sale a tobacco product, a product intended for use as a tobacco product, a product similar to a tobacco product, a product similar to a tobacco product or a component thereof. When the Advertising Act is amended, the ban on advertising provided for in § 17 also applies to a product intended for the use of a tobacco product and to a product and components similar to a tobacco product. The Penal Code supplements § 376 and adds prohibited tobacco products. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! This is a draft which will help to protect the health of young people in particular. I would like to emphasise once again that we do not want the use of tobacco products, including nicotine-free products, to normalise smoking as an acceptable standard of behaviour in society and create a favourable environment for starting tobacco products. Honourable members of the Riigikogu, I ask you to adopt the law!

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U21	We will move on to the 16th item, which is the first reading of the draft law amending the arms law initiated by the Government of the Republic and related amendments to other laws. Hanno Pevkuri, Minister of the Interior of the Speaking Chair of the Riigikogu, please present!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U22	Honourable Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! Dear members of the Riigikogu, good morning! A new day has begun, and I am pleased to introduce you to some other laws as an introduction to the civil society debate starting at 10 a.m. First of all, the law of arms. The draft before you is intended to resolve the problems encountered in the implementation of the arms law. The wording of the provisions will be updated and a number of provisions will be clarified in the interests of better understanding and correct interpretation. Due to amendments to the Arms Act, it is necessary to clarify the use of terms in the Strategic Goods Act. In addition, the Strategic Goods Act makes it more flexible to export service and professional weapons and ammunition from Estonia and to Estonia. The State Fees Act regulates the rates of state fees. Following a proposal by the Ministry of Defence, an amendment will be made to the Defence Union Act specifying how the active member of the Defence League can acquire ammunition. In addition, the wording of the Security Act will be amended so that a public oversight act may be drawn up in the future by an official in place of a police officer as provided for in the Act. Now a little bit more specific about the changes. First, the provisions on the granting, exchange and revocation of the acquisition permit and weapons permit are brought into line with the decisions of the Supreme Court. The Supreme Court found that the decision to issue a weapons permit or a weapons permit cannot be based solely on the fact that a person has been convicted of criminal offences or is suspected or accused in the circumstances arising from criminal proceedings. When deciding on a acquisition or weapons permit, the Supreme Court considers that account must be taken of the person who has been convicted of the criminal offence and the act he has committed and of the facts that are the subject of the charge in the case of a suspect or accused person. Secondly, I would point out the problem of not notifying the police or other authorities of non-fire weapons coming from other countries to Estonia and, therefore, it is not known whether the weapons brought to Estonia will be transferred to non-fire control or will be reactivated and placed on the so-called black market. In the draft, we propose a solution that the interested party must inform the police about the introduction of an unarmed weapon into Estonia. A document certifying that the weapon has been deactivated shall be issued in respect of the deactivated weapon. Provision shall also be made for misdemeanour penalties for non-compliance with the requirements for the handling of weapons which do not comply with the non-destructive requirements. A third important change is that weapons are authorised to be deactivated by private legal entities. Currently, it is foreseen that weapons will be deactivated and that persons checking the conformity of deactivated weapons will be authorised to repair or convert the weapon. According to the current standard, the weapon is declared deactivated by the Estonian Forensic Institute. The analysis shows that weapons reaching the EKEI meet the established requirements and that EKEI controls are therefore rather formal. The added value of the ECEI is mainly expressed in a guarantee of reliability, and that is because it is a public body. That is why the solution proposed in the draft is that weapons are subsequently recognised as unfit by a person authorised to repair or convert a weapon by issuing a certificate to that effect. This means that we will lose double control of the Forensic Institute and thereby increase the responsibility of the weapons masters. Fourthly, according to the legislation in force, the non-authorised handling of essential parts of non-processed firearms allows them to be brought to Estonia without police control and therefore there is no right to apply the sanction. After the processing of parts of the firearm which are not finished, a new illegal firearm not registered can be completed from these parts. The draft defines the components of a non-completed firearm as essential components. Liability shall be provided for cases where, in order to avoid restrictions laid down in the Weapons Act, the handling of essential parts of firearms which have been deliberately left unprocessed or essential parts of firearms which have not been deliberately deactivated in accordance with technical requirements is sought. Five. The acquisition of found and inherited weapons has also been simplified. Under the current rules, the acquisition of a weapon inherited or found is possible if the person holds a weapons permit. If the weapon is to be retained for remembrance rather than practical use, it is not necessary to burden the person and the police with applying for and processing the permit. Inherited weapons will be deposited with the police until the new owner decides what to do with the legacy. Because of the inability of the heirs to decide what to do with the weapon, a number of weapons have been concentrated in police warehouses. The proposal is to change this process. In order to facilitate the acquisition of a weapon inherited or found, a weapon found or inherited shall be allowed to be deactivated at the request of the new owner and by a decision of the police. Six. Only the Police and Border Guard Board has the right to carry out the destruction of weapons and ammunition on the basis of the current legal provisions. If criminal proceedings are conducted by the defence police and the court has decided to destroy the confiscated weapon, the defence police will be obliged to hand the weapon over to the Police and Border Guard Board, but it is unreasonable and time-consuming and not based on the particularities of the defence police's work. The proposal is therefore also to give the defence police the right to destroy the weapon on the basis of a court judgment. Seven. The export and transfer of service and professional weapons and ammunition from Estonia will become more flexible. The problem with current law is that the temporary withdrawal and introduction of service weapons and ammunition is subject to authorisation by the Minister concerned. However, in certain cases, such as personal protection, the removal of a service weapon must be decided very quickly. The permit procedure does not correspond to the nature of the police's duties, and that is why it is proposed that in the future the institution that withdraws the service weapon and ammunition from Estonia will send the Estonian Tax and Customs Board a notification stating the details of the shipment. Eighthly, I would point out that when issuing a weapons permit and a purchase permit, we will reduce unnecessary operations and change the rates of state fees to cover the costs of police operations. A few figures, too. In 2013, the police issued 3041 weapons acquisition permits and 8629 weapons permits. As regards the issue of a permit for acquisition and the issue of a permit for weapons, the current law requires the administrative decision of the prefect to be issued in addition to the permit. In order to reduce the administrative burden on the police, it is proposed to remove the obligation to issue an administrative decision. Instead of an administrative decision, the person shall be informed verbally of whether or not the person is granted an acquisition permit and a weapons permit after the examination has been carried out. The rates of state fees for activities carried out under the Arms Act have been unchanged for ten years. When the euro was introduced, the rates of state fees were converted from Estonian kroons into euros, but in essence the rates have not been adjusted or aligned with the actual costs. It is therefore also proposed to adjust the rates of state fees. Finally, there is a subject that, in the light of recent events, maybe also triggers some discussion in the Chamber. In particular, we propose that the conditions for storing weapons should be changed. In the case of individuals and natural persons, the current law provides that the weapon must in any case be kept at home, while the legal person must keep it at the place of storage which he or she has designated. According to the draft, it is also proposed to amend the conditions for holding a weapon to natural persons and to allow them, like legal persons, to hold a weapon at their own designated location, which will be notified to the Police and Border Guard Board and coordinated with the Agency. I believe that this will provide an opportunity to transfer the weapons of natural persons, if all conditions are met, to a much safer location. This could also reduce the risk that weapons held at home by private individuals could be used by those who are not entitled to do so. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U23	Minister, there are also questions for you. Ken-Marti Vaher, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U24	Thank you, Mr President. Here is a simple example where a private person has secure conditions at the workplace, such as a weapons locker, physical security for access to the legal person's location and electronic security. Such storage conditions are certainly much safer when a person is at home. As you know, you don't have to have a gun in your locker to hold a gun at home. Giving such an opportunity, provided that it is coordinated with the Police and Border Guard Board, which says whether or not deposit is safer, is necessary and reasonable in my opinion. The point is that it should not be restricted, the law should not say in the expression verb that a natural person's weapon must be in his place of residence, but may also be in a location other than that where the PBGB authorises it.

Despite that, this tragic event happened. Whether we come up with any changes between the first reading and the second reading, these thoughts are still open. Rather, we would also be happy to discuss with the Commission the ideas that have gathered in the Ministry and then see if we are going to change something further somewhere or not. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U29	Thank you! No further questions. I would like to ask Marko Pomerants, Vice-Chairman of the Legal Affairs Committee, to present the speech chair of the Riigikogu.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U30	Hello, dear Vice President. Ladies and gentlemen, those interested in the Arms Act! I have to say that the Minister did well to introduce the bill, and everything important in this bill was cleared. However, it is not the everyday interest of the members of the Committee on Legal Affairs to deal with the arms law, and that is why, in addition to what specifically concerns the draft, we have further questions during the debate. For example, the same question: If a person has only one weapon, should he have a weapons locker? It was explained on the spot that one weapon is primarily the responsibility of the owner of the weapon and that the obligation to have a locker should not be imposed on him by the legislator. There is no such obligation in the law either. The members of the Commission were interested in what happened to the granting of weapons permits to persons who had been convicted of criminal offences in the past. Tarmo Pruuli, adviser to the Ministry of the Interior, who assisted the Minister of Interior, replied that there may be automatic exclusion in certain cases, but for example in cases such as the offences against property, the police have discretion and can decide whether to grant or refuse authorisation. The Commission was also interested in whether the procedure for obtaining a medical certificate required for a driving licence could also be applied to a medical certificate for the issue of a weapons licence. It was explained to us in the Commission that a psychiatric examination is also required to obtain a medical certificate in the case of a weapons permit. Thus, these are medical certificates of different weights. The Commission was also asked about the European firearms passport. There was an interest in reconciling the European firearms passport with the national firearms passport so that two documents were not needed. It was explained that obtaining a European firearms passport was subject to the existence of a national firearms passport. The Commission also asked whether a person who has not been convicted of criminal offences would automatically be issued with a weapons permit. The answer was that the police had discretion as to whether or not to grant a person a weapons permit, but the decision to do so had to be justified. Interest was also expressed as to why it is forbidden to use silencer weapons in Estonia. It was explained that silencers are allowed, for example, in the shooting range, but the main reason for the ban is to ensure the effectiveness of public supervision. This issue has also been covered by another committee, namely the Committee on the Environment, in the framework of the draft Hunting Act. The Commission's debate, which was held on 20 October, unanimously found it reasonable to conclude the first reading in this Chamber. Should this happen, it is proposed that the deadline for amendments be set at 4 p.m. on 26 November 2014. I was appointed rapporteur.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U32	We will move on to item 17, which is the first reading of the Act amending the Act on Assistant Police and Traffic Act 724 initiated by the Government of the Republic. Hanno Pevkuri, Minister of the Interior of the Speaking Chair of the Riigikogu, please present!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U33	Honourable Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu and Chairman of the Riigikogu! It's good to see you on the remote together. Next, I'll introduce you to the law amending the law on assistant police officers and the law amending the law on traffic. As always, first the purpose of the draft and then the changes more precisely. In short, the aim of the draft before you is to make it easier to become an assistant police officer, the procedures for completing the training of an assistant police officer and the involvement of auxiliary police officers in police activities, including prevention. There are still some relatively technical changes and, as is already clear from the title of the draft, we are also amending the law on traffic. Now more about the changes. First of all, becoming an assistant cop. In order to make a substantive assessment of professional fitness, it is proposed to make becoming an assistant police officer similar to becoming a voluntary rescuer and a sea rescuer, in other words, to introduce a professional interview and to waive secondary education requirements. The draft also amends the language requirements of the assistant police officer. A person with B2 proficiency in the Estonian language can be called an Assistant Police Officer, but an Assistant Police Officer who carries out the task independently must continue to have C1 proficiency in the Estonian language. A B2 level of language proficiency does not reduce the ability of an assistant police officer to assist a police officer, given that a person undergoes the necessary training for police action, including obtaining professional terminology, and acts under the guidance of a police officer. Another important change concerns prevention. The aim of the amendment is to enable those who wish to contribute only to police prevention and/or police demonstration to act as a police assistant. Police contribute to prevention in areas such as traffic, violence, addictive substances, crimes against property, corruption and threats online. Persons who commit themselves to prevention do not have to undergo a medical check-up when they apply or during their service. They also do not have the right to use special equipment. Thirdly, I would like to point out that we are planning to make training more flexible and to facilitate the involvement of help police officers. More specifically, the grounds for regulating the handling of special equipment and weapons in the future, including compliance with the conditions and, where appropriate, the keeping of special equipment in the home of an auxiliary police officer living far from the permanent seat of the police, will be clarified. Paragraph 84 (1) (1) of the current Traffic Act defines an alarm vehicle. It provides that an alarm vehicle is a vehicle and an off-road vehicle carrying out urgent duties, duties or tasks during which it is necessary to warn other road users of the presence of such a vehicle. The draft replaces the term 'function' with the term 'public authority function', which gives a clear basis that, if necessary, a police officer may also transfer the driving of an alarm vehicle to an auxiliary police officer. Precept. Under the current law, the injunction may be drawn up by an assistant police officer with independent competence. According to the draft, this right will also be extended to an auxiliary police officer under the direction of a police officer. Security check. Under current law, an assistant police officer, both with independent competence and under the direction of the police, may carry out security checks where this is necessary to counter an imminent increased threat or where a person may be deprived of liberty under the law. The proposal gives the assistant police officer, acting under the direction of a police officer, the right to carry out security checks on the same basis as the police officer. The rights of an independent assistant police officer shall not be extended. A person's examination. The law in force does not give the Assistant Police Officer the right to apply this measure, but it would be reasonable to do so. According to the draft law, an auxiliary police officer may review a person's body, clothing, clothing, or wearing a body if: first, there are reasonable grounds to believe that a person carries with him or her an item or substance which may be taken for legal storage, occupation or confiscation; second, it is necessary to identify an increased risk when a person is present in or in the immediate vicinity of a building which is important for the functioning of public authority. Unlike a police officer, an assistant police officer does not have the right to review the person's cavities, nor does he have the right to review anyone for the purpose of establishing identity. An auxiliary police officer shall have the right to carry out security checks, which shall mean checking the person or his or her clothing by observation or touch or by technical means. However, if a security check suspects that a person is carrying a thing or substance that may be taken into custody, seized or confiscated under the law, the assistant police officer should be able to check it further and, as I mentioned, he/she needs the right to review the person. Checking out an outlaw. The right to apply this measure has not been granted to an assistant police officer under current law, but in practice it has become necessary to apply it. That is why it is proposed that an assistant police officer acting under the direction of a police officer be given the right to apply the measure on the same basis as a police officer. On the other hand, the rights to apply the aid police measure with independent competence are restricted. The latter is granted the right to apply the measure if the movable property is carried out by a person who may be subject to security checks under the law or may be subject to legal review. The need is due to the fact that an assistant police officer with independent competence already has the right to carry out security checks and the proposal also seeks to extend his or her rights to review the person. Entering the estate. According to the current wording, an auxiliary police officer acting under the direction of a police officer may enter, without the consent of the holder, a limited or marked immovable property, building, dwelling or space in his possession, thereby opening doors, gates and removing other obstacles, where this is necessary to identify or deter an increased threat. The amendment grants the same rights to the assistant police officer to enter the holding as a police officer. Checking out the estate. An auxiliary police officer acting under the supervision of a police officer may inspect the premises if there are reasonable grounds for believing that an immovable property, a building or a room, limited or marked, may be deprived of liberty under law or whose life, health or physical integrity is threatened by his or her helplessness. It is now a valid law. The amendment provides for the rights of the assistant police officer to be extended when examining the property and granted the same rights as the police officer. An auxiliary police officer with independent competence shall not have the right to apply the measure. Detention of property. Where, under the law in force, an auxiliary police officer may deposit an movable property identified in the course of a security check by which a person may endanger himself or another person or whose possession is prohibited by law, the assistant police officer shall also have the right to take over the case if it has been established in the course of the examination of the person. We will also stop by direct coercion and the use of a firearm. The draft specifies that an auxiliary police officer may use physical force, special equipment and weapon on the basis and in accordance with the procedures laid down in the law on law enforcement, taking into account the special features of the auxiliary police officer's law. It further provides that an auxiliary police officer is entitled to apply direct coercion under the direction of a police officer where this is strictly necessary to achieve the objective of the police officer's application of the national surveillance measure. Pursuant to § 3 (1) of the Act on Assistant Police Officers, the primary task is to assist the police in preventing, detecting, deterring and eliminating threats to public policy. The application of direct coercion is subject to the existence of a generally valid administrative act and, in most cases, the act should be delivered by a police officer. Since the role of the assistant police officer is to assist the police officer, the assistant police officer should be able to assist the police officer, if necessary, in the application of direct coercion in order to achieve the objective of the measure he applies. For example, a police officer has the right to apply a coercion to establish identity, but an auxiliary police officer does not have that right. It would be unreasonable, however, to give an assistant police officer the right to apply the measure if, in fact, he is responsible for assisting the police officer in applying it, if necessary. It is also unreasonable, for example, to extend the grounds for the right to detention by an auxiliary police officer himself when it comes to the regulation of the detention of a person. Under current law, an auxiliary police officer may only use a weapon for emergency protection purposes in the performance of an auxiliary police officer's task. However, the current practice must be taken into account: if the risk is due to the matter, the emergency rules must be followed if it is to be removed. This means that in the event of an animal attack, an auxiliary police officer will not be able to defend himself with a firearm. Given that a police officer and an auxiliary police officer who has the right to use a firearm have the same training, the draft provides that an assistant police officer is entitled to defend himself against an animal attack by means of a firearm. Finally, some organisational changes. There's no need for an auxiliary cop's badge. Assistant police officers are obliged to wear a safety vest in the performance of their duties and may also use police uniforms, which are usually done. The bill is about losing the badges of an assistant police officer. Due to the desire to create clarity on the reasons for the release, § 42 (4) of the Auxiliary Police Act, which provides for the death of an Auxiliary Police Officer as an additional basis for the release from the status, is added. The Auxiliary Police Act will be supplemented by the Public Service Act and the terms used to date will be replaced by new ones. First, the current reference to the Code of Ethics of the Civil Service Act annexed to the Civil Service Act is replaced by a reference to the Code of Ethics of the Official. Second, the term 'permanent partner' is replaced by the term'marriage partner' used in the Civil Service Act. We support the Committee on Legal Affairs' proposal to conclude the first reading and, hopefully, to adopt the amendments as a law. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U40	The next item is the first reading of the draft law amending the Citizenship Act and State Fees Act 737 initiated by the Government of the Republic. Anno Pevkur, Minister of the Interior, will make a presentation on behalf of the initiators. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U41	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Allow me to introduce you to the draft law amending the Citizenship Act and the State Fees Act. The draft is intended to introduce changes that can be divided into three blocks. The first major change is that in the future the minor cannot be deprived of his or her nationality. The current Citizenship Act provides that a person who, in addition to Estonian citizenship, also acquires the citizenship of another state by birth must, within three years of becoming 18, choose whether he or she will renounce the citizenship of Estonia or another state. Thus, in addition to Estonian citizenship, the multi-citizenship of a person who has acquired the citizenship of another country is now legitimate until his 21st year of life. The draft proposes to amend § 3 of the Citizenship Act in such a way that all persons who acquire or receive Estonian citizenship as a minor as well as the citizenship of another state must give up the citizenship of either Estonia or another state within three years of becoming 18 years of age. Thus, the draft provides that children who have received Estonian citizenship by naturalisation are in the same situation as children who acquired Estonian citizenship at birth and that this rule applies equally to both Estonian citizens at birth and persons who have received Estonian citizenship by naturalisation, inter alia, whether the citizenship of another state has been acquired before or after the acquisition of Estonian citizenship. The second amendment is that applicants for citizenship aged 65 or over will have to pass only an oral language examination in the future. If, according to the law in force, the person applying for Estonian citizenship before 1 January 1930 is exempt from the requirement that the person must be able to produce a simple text on an familiar or self-interested subject, this is the written part of the Estonian language examination. According to the draft, the exemption from the written part of the examination is linked to the age of the person instead of the date of birth. I would like to draw attention to two points here. First, when the current Citizenship Act was adopted, the exemption from the written examination was related to people who were 65 years of age and older at the time of the entry into force of the Act, but then this connection was created through their date of birth and the fixed date of birth, as stated, was 1 January 1930. The Citizenship Act entered into force on 1 April 1995. As a result of the government's discussion, it was considered that it would be more reasonable and appropriate not to take into account the date of birth but to fix the age limit. The statistics for recent years show that there have been about ten people over the age of 65 who have received citizenship each year. The second point I would like to emphasise once again is that, in any case, there will also be an obligation for people aged 65 or over to take the oral part of the language examination. According to the draft currently under discussion, they will only be released from the written part of the examination. As a third amendment, I mention the cessation of the reproduction of citizenshiplessness. According to the current law, parents of undefined citizenship can apply for Estonian citizenship for their children born in Estonia in a simplified manner, if the parents have been legally resident in Estonia for at least five years at the time of submitting their application and no country considers them to be a citizen under the laws in force. The draft provides for the opposite situation where a minor under 15 years of age, born in Estonia and moving permanently to Estonia immediately after birth with his or her parents, receives Estonian citizenship under naturalisation from the moment of birth if his or her parents or parent raising the child alone, who is not considered by any state to be a citizen under the laws in force, have been legally resident in Estonia for at least five years at the time of birth. At the same time, the parents will be left with the opportunity to give up the child's Estonian citizenship thus obtained within one year. Due to the amendment of the law, the number of children of undefined citizenship who are entitled to Estonian citizenship would not increase, but as a result of the amendment of the law, children who would otherwise be expected to remain without Estonian citizenship or residence permit due to their parents' lack of activity. The amendment does not therefore increase the number of entitled persons, but would only change the procedure. For parents, it becomes easier and more comfortable because they no longer have to apply for Estonian citizenship for the child. In addition to the above amendments, the draft provides for other changes to simplify procedures for applying for citizenship, allow for more flexibility in the implementation of the law and brings the Citizenship Act into line with other laws. For example, the six-month waiting period following the submission of an application for the acquisition or restoration of Estonian citizenship is abandoned. The bill replaces the requirement to live in Estonia just before applying for Estonian citizenship for five years consecutively with the requirement to live permanently in Estonia for at least five years for eight years. The requests for the acquisition of Estonian citizenship, the lists of data to be provided and the documents to be added in the current Act are also replaced by a mandate rule for the Minister of the Interior. It is unwise to provide for them at the level of the law in circumstances where they may need to be amended more often than the law itself. As the initiator of the draft act and as the representative of the Government of the Republic, I support the proposal of the Committee on Constitutional Affairs to conclude the first reading today and to adopt the draft as a law in the future. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U74	Mr President-in-Office of the Council. Today, citizenship is not only a sign on paper, such as a woman or a man, but a definition of who I am and where I belong. As stated in the executive edition of the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia, the establishment of rules relating to citizenship policy, especially naturalisation, is largely a matter of political choice. Let us see what choices have been made in the context of the draft under discussion. First of all, the current government has chosen a path that gives children under 18 years of age the right to Estonian citizenship automatically, provided that one of the parents has Estonian citizenship or both parents are stateless persons or the child has only one parent and the parent is a stateless person. This approach guarantees the child the right to be a citizen of a country designated by his or her parent. If the parents have not appointed it, the child will become a citizen of the country of birth. On the one hand, I understand the purpose of such principles, but on the other hand, this is an unfair situation for children who have one of their parents, for example, Russian citizenship and the other is a stateless person. Only children of Russian citizenship continue to be born into such a family, despite the fact that children are born in Estonia, go to school here and live according to the principles of the Estonian state. The Centre Party considers that these children must also be entitled to Estonian citizenship automatically, without any conditions. Secondly, the issue of double citizenship still remains confusing. Although this draft provides an exception for children under 18 years of age, it is still a choice to dissuade them from their citizenship. And this is contrary to the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia. This means that our constitution does not allow the citizenship acquired by birth to be taken away from anyone. Nevertheless, in the future, a child who has double citizenship will be obliged to decide whether to remain a citizen of one country or another when he or she reaches the age of 18. For example, if one parent of the child is Estonian and the other is Russian, he or she is most likely to have two citizenships when he or she becomes an adult. He's both born. This means that the child is forced to give up one or another nationality. The obligation to make a choice is also a withdrawal within the meaning of the Constitution, because it is not possible to retain both nationalityes. I'm asking for more time!

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U78	Good President! Mart Nutt explained this principle a little in his article today when he said that avoiding exceptions and excluding individual procedures was a political choice that might no longer be justified in the current situation. Therefore, the problems associated with the Kohtla-Järve girl, the retired soldier or perhaps even dozens of people who have been caught by the gears should be solved somehow, in a different way, in a more human way, not in principle. Of course, the law must not be evil, and the evil law must be changed, but changing the evil law does not require the goal-kicking. We read from quite the first page of the explanatory memorandum to the current draft that the draft relates to the points of the Reform Party and the Coalition Agreement between the Social Democratic Party. That is, in fact, the only reason for this bill. A few pages forward it has been said that this same coalition agreement, and it seems to me that the forthcoming elections, too, have included the urgency of the draft procedure. The word 'quick nature' isn't my design, it's taken from this very explanatory note. What, then, is the cost of this compromise? Citizenship policy has four basic principles: blood, not birth, polycitizenship, naturalisation on the basis of a declaration of intent, and language requirement. It is not a spite, it is an instrument to ensure compliance with the principles laid down in the preamble to the Constitution. All these four principles this bill dilutes, changes in principle. So I get the impression that instead of resolving individual cases, we have unfortunately given in to interests and forces, which were not supposed to serve the interests of the Republic of Estonia in the best possible way, as well as a better understanding of our party. I therefore propose, on behalf of the Group of the Fatherland and Res Publica Union, to reject the draft law amending the Citizenship Act and the State Fees Act 737 at first reading.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U84	Our next item is the first reading of the draft law amending the Law on Safety at Sea and the Law on State Fees initiated by the Government of the Republic. The initiator will be presented by Urve Palo, Minister of Economic Affairs and Infrastructure. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U85	Good morning, Mr. Chairman. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! This draft amends the Maritime Safety Act and the State Fees Act. First of all, I will talk about the amendments to the law on maritime safety. The amendments to the Maritime Safety Act relate in particular to the alignment of national law with the relevant European Union legislation, directives. The rules for keeping the passenger list on national shipping lines, the regulation for the issue of security inspections and security certificates and the technical implementing provisions concerning the admissibility of the marketing and installation of marine equipment shall be taken over. I'll tell you more about the changes. In accordance with Directive 98/41/EC, for passenger ships carrying out voyages of more than 20 nautical miles, a personal register must be established and coordinated with the Maritime Administration. The purpose of this amendment is to improve the safety of passengers and crew and the possibilities for rescue on passenger ships moving between ports of the European Union and to ensure that search and rescue work and dealing with the consequences of a possible accident are more effective. For domestic passenger transport only the Ringsu-Roomassaare and Ringsu-Pärnu lines are subject to the requirement. All other lines, such as shipping lines between the mainland and Muhu County and between the mainland and Hiiumaa, fall outside the scope because they are less than 20 nautical miles long. In the case of the Väinamere Lines of the Ruhnu shipping company, the system has not been formally established, but it has now been technically developed and the costs of establishing the register have been reimbursed to the company from the state budget. Second. Pursuant to Directive 2012/35/EU of the European Union, the permissible alcoholic strength shall be reduced during the watchkeeping service of the ship. The Directive provides that, in order to prevent alcohol abuse, Member States shall establish a maximum alcoholic strength of 0,5 milligrams in one gram of blood or 0,25 milligrams in one litre of breath for masters, officers and other seafarers to carry out their duties in the field of maritime safety, maritime security and protection of the marine environment. At present, the corresponding rates are 0.8 milligrams per gram of blood and 0.4 milligrams per litre of air. A similar change will be introduced for recreational craft. According to the Maritime Safety Act, both the person present in the watchkeeping service of the ship and the driver of the recreational craft are responsible for ship safety and navigation, which means that the limits imposed on them are equivalent, i.e. both in the current law and on the basis of the draft. In the case of recreational craft, practice has shown that the number of boat accidents caused by alcohol drinking remains relatively high (including cases where people were rescued) and has shown some upward trend in 2014. In addition, the introduction of the amendments takes into account the fact that recreational craft can be used for leisure trips and for up to 12 persons, which is why the reduction of the permitted limits for recreational craft drivers is also aimed at ensuring greater passenger safety. A little bit of an amendment to the state fee law. The main objective of the amendments to the State Fees Act is to simplify the procedures for the calculation of state fees relating to the technical supervision of ships. The draft removes the existing specifications of a ship classified or not, the age-related coefficients of the ship, etc., and transfers the current model for calculating the state fee based on the length of the ship to the calculation scheme based on the working hours of the supervising officer. On the basis of the 2010 amendment to the State Fees Act, the state fee for the technical inspection of the ship is currently calculated on the basis of the length of the ship, i.e. the metres. The current scheme does not take into account the technical complexity and condition of the vessels. This led in practice to multiple price increases and was, at the same time, too burdensome for certain types of vessels, floating equipment. As a result, there were often situations where the practical use of several floating devices was abandoned. The Chancellor of Justice has also pointed out the problems of the current methodology for calculating state fees in the case of mobile floating equipment in his memo. As a result, the draft has changed from the overall length of the ship to the survey time scheme on the basis of the scheme for calculating the state fee. Thus, the technical complexity of the ship and the condition of the ship become more important. The smaller the time taken for the technical inspection, the lower the state fee. This principle is also fairer with regard to the shipowner. In particular, the change will have a positive impact on owners of historic vessels, mobile floating equipment and smaller fishing vessels. The inspection of such ships is not complex due to the lack of technical equipment and navigational equipment and the size of the supervising officer is smaller than that of large ships, which should lead to a significant reduction in the state fee paid for the new calculation. This amendment is also important for the development of Estonian maritime heritage. All Estonian historic ships have a passenger transport certificate, which means that, according to the Maritime Safety Act, they must have at least five full inspections and two dock inspections during a five-year period. Given that the owners of historic ships are primarily business-loving maritime enthusiasts, one of the objectives of which is to restore marine culture, high fees may give rise to a setback to the development of this sector. The bill adjusts the rates of state fees for the issue of some diplomas and certificates for crew members. Most of these state fees have been set in November 2008 and have remained unchanged until now. At the same time, the real costs of printing and issuing documents have increased considerably, as illustrated by the increase in the consumer price index. The state fee for diplomas and certificates for crew members will increase to a small extent, correspondingly EUR 5 up to now EUR 3.19 and EUR 15 instead of the current EUR 12.78. In this regard, I would like to point out that, since the Riigikogu is at the same time in the process of preparing the full text of the new State Fees Act 725, which should enter into force on 1 January 2015, the amendments to the State Fees Act will be dealt with within the framework of the above-mentioned draft. The Riigikogu has also tabled an amendment to this effect to the Committee on Monetary Affairs. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U110	Our next item is the first reading of the draft Act amending the Aviation Act and the State Fees Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. Urve Palo, Minister of Economic Affairs and Infrastructure, will make his presentation. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U111	Dear Chairman, Brave colleagues! Especially many brave ones, I see, are from the Reform Party. It's 3:30. I'm glad you're spending time here with me. It's an honor and a pleasure. This draft introduces amendments to the Aviation Act and the State Fees Act. In particular, the draft aims to increase aviation safety and to raise the level of aviation safety culture in general, taking into account European Union law, international civil aviation standards and recommendations, as well as operational bottlenecks in the aviation sector. First of all, the amendments to the Aviation Act. First of all, according to the draft, the regulation of the rules on flights established in Estonia at national level is repealed. Instead, the common rules of the European Union will apply from 4 December 2014, as well as national rules and derogations from European regulation, as well as differences from the European Commission's implementing regulation. Secondly, the draft has designated the Authority as the competent authority within the meaning of the implementing regulations adopted pursuant to Regulation No 216/2008 of the European Union, unless otherwise provided for in the law. This approach eliminates the need to amend the aviation law every time the new implementing rules enter into force. Thirdly, two of the standards set out in the Annexes to the Convention on International Civil Aviation concerning the national aviation safety programme and the simplification scheme will be transposed. The aim of the National Aviation Safety Programme is to organise aviation safety related areas at national level, while supporting the harmoniously efficient development of all air transport. More broadly, there is national interest, as well as the principle of the Aviation Safety Programme, to ensure coordinated and effective monitoring of the management of the risks already identified and likely to arise in aviation safety, so that the development and diversity of air transport are not disrupted. Fourthly, national regulation is introduced for aircraft not certified by the European Aviation Safety Agency (EASA). These are, for example, An-2, L-39 and partly balloons. The development of the regulation takes account of the fact that European Union regulations regulate primarily airworthiness requirements for aircraft used in international commercial air transport or other commercial operations, while non-EASA aircraft are primarily operated for private purposes. In view of the purpose of the operation, the requirements for the continuing airworthiness of non-EASA aircraft and the requirements for persons involved in its organisation are somewhat less stringent than those for the continuing airworthiness of aircraft used in commercial air transport. These aircraft are included in the aircraft register maintained by the Authority around 60. A total of 169 aircraft are included in the register. Fifthly, different aspects of aviation security are being changed in the light of the European Union legal framework. For example, the procedures for recognising a regulated supplier of in-flight supplies, a known consignor, a regulated agent and a security instructor are regulated. These are persons responsible for the application of security measures for cargo, mail and in-flight supplies. Sixthly, as a new issue, the law regulates the obligation to certify an air procedures provider. This is a person who develops standard instrument arrival, instrument approach or instrument departure procedures for aircraft. The change was due to the deficiencies identified during the ICAO aviation safety audit in May 2010, according to which Estonia does not have a legal basis for national supervision of the operator of aviation procedures. The above provisions establish and monitor a legal framework under which the Authority will be able to certify and supervise flight procedures professionals if the procedures are in accordance with the established conditions. Seventhly, as a new task, the Authority will monitor compliance with the parachute requirements by means of a manual describing the parachute operations. The aim of the amendment is to increase safety levels in the organisation and execution of parachute jumps. The draft has also introduced other changes due to the problems encountered in practice and the need to modernise regulation. For example, the working and rest requirements of civil aircraft crew members have been substantially improved or specified. As regards the amendments to the State Fees Act, they set the fees for the conduct of the following procedures: recognition of the security instructor, recognition of the person applying security measures, recognition of the regulated supplier of in-flight supplies, recognition of the known consignor, recognition of the regulated agent, application for coordination of the Code of Conduct for Parachuting Operations and examination of the application for the issue, amendment and extension of the certificate of the flight procedures officer. That's all. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U118	Our next item is the first reading of the draft Act 748 on the ratification of the Council of Europe Convention on Trafficking in Human Beings initiated by the Government of the Republic. Andres Anvelt, Minister of Justice, will make a presentation from the initiator's side. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U119	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! Good morning, then, for me too. I thank everyone for the opportunity to be the anchor of your very important sitting today. The Government has submitted a draft act ratifying the Council of Europe Convention against Trafficking in Human Beings to the Riigikogu for discussion. The purpose of this draft is to ratify the above-mentioned convention and then to implement it later in Estonia. The Convention was opened for signature already on 16 May 2005 in Warsaw. Today it has been signed by 43 and ratified by 41 Member States of the Council of Europe. Estonia and the Czech Republic are the only Member States of the European Union that have not yet ratified the Convention. The bill has a direct impact on the fulfilment of Estonia's international obligations. Since Estonia signed the Convention on Trafficking in Human Beings already four years ago, the expectation of joining it is also very high. As we know, in recent decades, the European Union has become a destination for immigrants from different continents. In this context, the pressure on Estonia's borders is also increasing, which is why ratification of this convention is vital. As we know, illegal immigration is linked to cross-border crime, particularly smuggling of people, labour slavery, etc. Our good geopolitical location is also economically a risk factor that pressure on Estonia's borders as a transit country will increase in the future. The Convention has three broad objectives. Firstly, to prevent and combat trafficking in human beings. Secondly, to protect the rights of victims of trafficking in human beings, to establish a general set of standards for the protection and assistance of victims and witnesses. The Convention also seeks to ensure effective investigation and prosecution. The third objective is to promote international cooperation against trafficking in human beings. Since the requirements of the Convention are largely in line with those of the European Directive on Trafficking in Human Beings, which we have already amended several laws and trainings in this area, there is no longer any need for additional time to adapt to the requirements of the Convention. In Estonia's circumstances, these objectives have mostly been transposed through the Victim Assistance Act, the Penal Code and the Procedural Laws, as well as through several development plans and planned measures. So I ask you to support this bill!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U120	Any questions for the initiator? No, it's not. Thank you, Minister. The Commission's rapporteur is Jüri Morozov. Please!

All the decisions were consensual. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U123	Our next item is the draft law amending the Penal Code and the Code of Criminal Procedure, initiated by the Government of the Republic, 751. Andres Anvelt, Minister of Justice, will make the opening statement. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U124	Thank you. I will then continue my mission in the Night's Riigikogu and introduce you to the draft law amending the Penal Code and the Code of Criminal Procedure. This is a very important draft for both security and day-to-day policy. The aim is to prevent crimes against the state, the preparation of which has taken place outside the Republic of Estonia, and to ensure a faster and more effective response to the more dangerous crimes at the border. The amendments will also provide a better opportunity to deal with such crimes. This draft has also been accompanied by one of the obligations of the Council of Europe under the Convention against Trafficking in Human Beings, which has not yet been regulated in our laws, which is to reduce the possibility of keeping people in so-called document custody. As we know, and as I mentioned in my previous speech, pressure on our external borders has increased and the number of registered crimes has increased in the irregular crossing of the state border or temporary control line of the Republic of Estonia. In order to ensure that security is not only enhanced by physical protection, but also by legal means, i.e. legal deterrents, this draft has been put into effect. New year's illegal border crossing would then be punishable as a criminal offence against the state, if it involves violence or threats, deprivation of liberty from another person, or if property is destroyed or violated to facilitate the illegal crossing of the border. For example, it would be a crime in the future if a border guard dog was injured by illegal border crossings, a border surveillance camera or a border building was broken. Illegal border crossings will continue to be criminally punishable even if the aim is to commit other crimes against the state in Estonia. Illegal border crossings are aggravating circumstances, for example when a cross-border player is carrying a weapon or someone's life is in danger. In the future, it will be possible to punish a person with imprisonment of up to 10 years if there are aggravating circumstances. As I mentioned, in order to prevent trafficking in human beings, provisions have been added to the draft, which criminalise the subsequent acquisition of a falsified document and the detention of another person's personal document, as well as provisions providing for responsibility for the destruction, violation, theft and concealment of an important personal document, and for the use and use of a falsified important personal document. In addition, the Code of Criminal Procedure will be supplemented by allowing evidence of illegal crossing of the state border and temporary control line of the Republic of Estonia to be collected using surveillance procedures when committed by a group. In order to ensure border security, it is necessary for the operators to be able to act in an operational manner and, where necessary, to use discreet surveillance tools. The parts of the draft law relating to illegal border crossing and surveillance activities in this respect are intended to enter into force on 2 January 2015 as from the date of entry into force of the so-called Penal Code Revision. The provisions on counterfeiting and retention of documents are intended to enter into force in general. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U125	Thank you. There are no questions. The Commission's rapporteur is Kalle Jents. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U126	Good morning, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, This draft was discussed by the Committee on Legal Affairs on 3 November. The minister gave an overview as comprehensive as today in the large hall of the Riigikogu and the members of the committee had no doubts about supporting this draft. The following decisions were taken: to put the draft on the plenary agenda on 12 November, which has now become 13 November (this was a consensual decision), to propose that the first reading be closed and that the deadline for amendments be set at 16 a.m. on 26 November. It was decided to appoint Mr Kalle Jents as rapporteur.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U127	There are no questions. The first reading of Bill 751 is complete. I will set the deadline for submitting amendments at 16 a.m. on 26 November.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U128	And we have reached the first reading of the draft law amending the law on law enforcement, which is the most appealing to our minds and minds. The draft has been initiated by the Government of the Republic. Please, on behalf of the initiators, Minister of Justice Andres Anvelt!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U129	Dear Riigikogu! Head of the Riigikogu! The last draft law I will present today is the draft law amending the law on law enforcement. The purpose of this draft is to bring the point of law enforcement law on alcohol consumption in public places into line with society's expectations and people's normal behaviour. A study carried out by the Institute for Health Development, which took place after 1 July, showed that 82% of the population did not support alcohol consumption in public places. Already in early autumn, the Ministry of Social Affairs and the Ministry of Justice launched a public debate. There was also a round table on the Law Enforcement Act, before which we called for proposals on the disputed provisions of the Law Enforcement Act. More than 50 proposals were made by organisations and individuals, most of whom considered that alcohol consumption in public should still be limited. That is how we decided to do it in the government and send you a bill to restore the regulation in force before July, i.e. a general ban on drinking alcohol in public places. It retains a part of the regulation that prohibits the use of alcoholic beverages or allows local authorities to consume alcoholic beverages in places related to children and to the promotion of health. In drawing up the draft, it has been taken into account that, under current law, local authorities have a wide range of possibilities to regulate the sales and consumption of alcoholic beverages, such as the right to restrict the sale of alcoholic beverages, to impose requirements for the organisation and maintenance of public events. The draft also extends the competence of the local government by being empowered by a general arrangement to determine the public places where alcohol consumption is permitted, and these places must then be marked accordingly. Amendments to the Law on Law Enforcement and not to the Law on Alcohol have been introduced in connection with the restriction of alcohol consumption, since the adoption of the Law on Law Enforcement has been decided years ago that alcohol consumption in public places is a matter of law enforcement rather than a matter of national alcohol policy. This bill is based on the same understanding. The municipality will be given the opportunity to specify the places and events where alcohol consumption is permitted. And as I mentioned, the general ban will still remain in force, and alcohol consumption cannot be allowed by the local authorities in those places relating to children, such as the proximity of playgrounds, health care establishments, public transport and related matters. Alcoholic beverages may be consumed in the future where the local authority has authorised the retail sale of the alcoholic beverage for consumption on the spot and at public events, subject to such authorisation. It is very important to note that these authorisations remain subject to the general requirement laid down in the Law Enforcement Act that where alcohol consumption is permitted, it should not disturb others. In addition to alcohol, the use of a non-food drink-producing substance in a public place shall be prohibited. Examples of such substances are, as we know, various liquids used in industry or manufacturing. The proposed amendment to the draft law is intended to enter into force in general terms. The sooner Parliament deals with the draft, the sooner alcohol consumption can be restricted in public. Thank you for your attention.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U130	As there are no questions for the Minister, I would like to ask the rapporteur for the committee, Neeme Suur.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U131	Mr. Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, The Committee on Legal Affairs dealt with the draft 752 on Monday, 3 November. Andres Anvelt, Minister of Justice, and Sander Põllumäe, Adviser to the Public Law Service of the Ministry of Justice, introduced the draft and described the objectives and regulatory objects of the Act. We also had a debate on this issue, in particular on issues concerning the competence of local governments. First of all, the question was whether the local authority could or should have the right to make any distinctions with regard to this restriction. The granting of the right to make distinctions was considered to be appropriate. A discussion also arose on the third point of the draft, which concerns the repeal of legislation given by the municipality. The question was whether this point was necessary and what self-determination rights stemming from the autonomy of the local authority would be. In response, it was found that such a declaration of invalidity was not a first time and was necessary for reasons of legal clarity. At the end of the discussion, procedural decisions were taken: it was proposed that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda on 12 November and that the first reading be closed and, if the first reading is closed, that the deadline for amendments be set at 16 a.m. on 26 November 2014. Neeme the Great was appointed rapporteur. All procedural decisions were taken in a consensual manner. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U196	Thank you, manager. Prime Minister! I'm asking about hidden taxation. ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U197	Thank you. As an electricity consumer, you and I have no reason to worry about buying gas routes at the expense of electricity. This represents a relatively small part of the gas tariff, as the total cost of the gas network is unknown. The fact that Elering acquires majority ownership of Eesti Gaas's core network does not in itself increase the gas tariff in any way. Elering receives money in consumer payments. If, until now, payments have been received by the companies from which Elering buys its stake, then further payments will be received by Elering, who will receive the money back from it. We have reached the point that the gas core network, like the electricity core network, will soon become part of the strategic infrastructure of the Estonian state, which is under the control of the Estonian state. I really think it's a big deal. I thank Elering and the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications for their very good work. Within seven months, it was possible to reach the actual transaction and make a decision, which will undoubtedly increase the confidence of Estonian gas consumers. We know very well that gas has also been used as a political weapon. The fact that the gas core network is under the control of the Estonian state gives confidence to gas consumers and is therefore a very important step, not only in gas policy, but also in security policy.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U201	Thank you for your question. Fuel oil is now known to be one of the most expensive fuel types for domestic heating and hot water, if not the most expensive. Its price is more or less the same as electricity. Indeed, the state wants to help people to gradually switch to cheaper heating methods, which are ultimately much more favourable to their wallets. The country will cease to differentiate between imported fuel and oil, namely special labelling, which is currently making the price more favourable. It will disappear from May 1, next year. As the heating period begins in the autumn, people will feel the impact of this change more in the autumn of next year. We have already developed a measure to support families. In this way, they can prepare to replace their existing oil boilers with more up - to - date and ultimately more wallet - friendly ones. From the beginning of the new year, you can apply for a grant to replace your oil boiler through KredEx. The maximum amount of the application is EUR 4000 per applicant and up to 40% of the total cost of the project, which is a pretty large grant. A similar service is provided by KIK, which provides up to 50% support to housing associations so that they can also exchange their oil boilers for more energy-efficient and cheaper ones.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U210	The next item is the second reading of draft 734 of the Riigikogu's decision 'Creating the European Stability Mechanism to create a tool for the direct capitalisation of banks' presented by the Government of the Republic. The rapporteur of the Monetary Commission is Aivar Sõerd. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U211	Honourable Chairman! Ladies and gentlemen, Riigikogu Committee on Finance...
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U212	Wait a minute, I'm sorry! [[The manager's ringing the bell.]] Thank you, ambassadors! Please, Aivar!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U213	The Committee on Finance discussed this draft decision at its meeting on 4 November. It should be recalled here that the European Stability Mechanism, the ESM, was set up to guarantee the stability of the euro area and started work already in October 2012. The content of this draft is the approval by the Riigikogu of the creation of an additional support tool within the ESM, which is the direct recapitalisation of banks. Through the European Stability Mechanism, the Bank's direct recapitalisation instrument will allow direct investment in the share capital of financial institutions in difficulty, together with the Member State concerned and under specific conditions. This will reduce the link between public debt and bank risk, which has had a negative impact on the economies of the euro area. The instrument also provides an additional opportunity to cover the capital shortfalls identified during the assessment of the banks prior to the transition to a single banking supervision. A ceiling of EUR 60 billion has been set for this instrument. The measure would itself apply to the exhaustion of other remedies in a crisis situation where private capital, i.e. the conversion of liabilities and claims rights and the resolution tools collected from banks' payments, are not sufficient to restore a specific public authority and where the high indebtedness of the country in which the problem bank is located does not allow ESM to intervene in the normal manner. No proposal for amendments to this draft decision was received by the deadline. As has been said, this draft was dealt with by the Commission at its meeting on 4 November and did not change the draft. Nor was there a substantive debate in the Commission, and the Commission decided by consensus to propose a final vote on the draft. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U224	The next item is the second reading of draft law 728 amending the Law on study grants and student loans initiated by the Government of the Republic and related amendments to other laws. The rapporteur for the Committee on Culture is Mart Meri. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U225	Ladies and gentlemen, The draft law amending the Study Allowances and Student Loan Act 728 was indeed initiated by the Government of the Republic in September of this year. As a reminder, the draft aims to introduce a new type of education allowance, a specific grant based on the need, and, secondly, to lower the requirement to meet the study volume from 100% to 75%, which is a minimum requirement for full-time learning. The first reading of the bill took place here on 15 October. No proposals were received from the members of the Riigikogu and the groups of delegates by the deadline for submitting amendments by 29 October. However, three proposals were submitted by the Ministry of Education and Research and the Committee on Culture formulated two proposals. All five amendments were unanimously approved by the Commission in the discussions held on 3 and 5 November. The explanatory memorandum to the second reading of the bill is quite comprehensive. I will not introduce the amendments in detail; I will briefly mention them. The first proposal clarifies the list of conditions for applying for the specific support. The second amendment clarifies the period during which the special allowance was granted. The third amendment is an implementing provision which obliges educational institutions to introduce a special scheme by 1 February 2015 at the latest. The fourth amendment would introduce that the allowances paid from the Special Support Fund set up by the institution are not included in the definition of family incomes for the purpose of determining the payment of income support. This is an important point. Amendment 5 is purely technical. On 3 October, the Committee on Culture decided to submit a draft for the second reading of 728 on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu by 12 November and to propose that the second reading be terminated. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U230	Ladies and gentlemen, I will speak for one reason only in the case of second reading. In social media, which students and other young people use a lot, this bill has also been discussed very nicely, but there are two misconceptions, and I will use this arena to correct them. Firstly, it is not quite right to say that this is a perfectly unfair draft or some other groundbreaking changes. It's not like that. The need-based study grants introduced to the Riigikogu by the Isamaa and Res Publica Union and the Reform Party coalition were really a systemic change. This draft improves some of the shortcomings of the original draft. However, I would remind you that even when we discussed this for the first time in this Chamber, we all agreed that since this is the first need-based system in the history of the Republic of Estonia, it needs to be further developed later. These changes to this draft concern, as the previous speaker said, a different procedure for calculating social benefits. A reduction in the required volume of study to 75% has also been included in the draft, which universities could still do themselves, and mostly in practice. So there are changes. These changes are in a positive direction, but they are not a new system or a groundbreaking draft. Universities have also paid special grants so far. These specific grants were already in place before the need-based learning grants. What is positive about this bill is that it separates the special allowances paid from the income that is written in the calculation of income support, i.e. it allows a young person to obtain both, if necessary, which is absolutely fair and correct. I also underline that this system has not yet been completed so that the Riigikogu can be fully satisfied with it. This was also acknowledged by the Committee on Culture at its meetings. The same simple example that if an adult student has a parent who has paid him a living but has lived away from his family, his income is now included in the student's income, although the same parent no longer naturally has to pay the student a living and mostly does not. Another example that went through the debate is the question of siblings, a situation where there is a family rich in children and where adult siblings earn income. In Estonian society in general, it is not the custom that siblings necessarily maintain each other together. I recommend that everyone vote in favour of this draft at third reading, but I strongly recommend that the Riigikogu proceed with the development of this system and finally find sufficient flexible solutions to help young people with educational interests. Once again, as a reminder, according to the 'Eurostudent IV' survey, the most employed students in Estonia are children of both rich and poor families. If we can help the children of poorer families more flexibly, all proposals from students themselves and from various institutions will, of course, continue to be welcome. Thank you very much.
