ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U240	Thank you very much. Good ambassadors! I would simply like to recall that the payment of funeral allowances was waived on 18 June 2009 and that this new regime became applicable already after 12 days. No adjustment time was granted. The authorities then had the Reform Party and the IRL, the Greens supported them with their votes. The amount of the funeral allowance was EUR 191,74. The amount that was won, if you could say so at all, was EUR 3.5 million in total. That's how much coffin money was saved. Hanno Pevkur, Minister of Social Affairs at the time, quite strangely explained the reasons for giving up the grant: the government wants to increase the efficiency of using the money. He explained that restricting the number of beneficiaries of funeral grants to municipalities and municipalities that organise funerals for unfamiliar people is not a temporary solution, but is simply an opportunity to make better use of money. They didn't even put the need behind us, they said it had to be lost. Just as dental benefits and free home delivery of pensions were abolished, and so many more. I would very much like to stress one fact to you. Money for funeral allowance was found in the young Republic of Estonia. Regulation No 190 was published on 23 June 1993. This was a completely new Republic of Estonia and the annual budget, by the way, was 262 million euros, or 4.1 billion kroons. At present, our state budget is EUR 8.4 billion. I repeat: EUR 262 million was in 1993. Right now, we're basically just giving the cripples an excuse for expressing themselves! And it's as if we're proud that it's there again. Of course, it is a good thing that this too little will come, this 'need-based support', but it is essentially just a matter of labelling. Many people who are not exposed to it may have the impression that funeral support will be restored. It's nice, the government's working, it's like it's upstairs. In fact, we should pay the death grant exactly as some other support, which is considered to be the result of the fact. One example is the birth grant. The funeral is very expensive. It's expensive for everyone. Even a very moderate funeral is very, very expensive for a middle-income person. Older people often don't have that money anymore, and even if they do, the family doesn't get it right away, the coffin money isn't being held under the pillow, it has to be in the bank. So it was a very ugly cut. However, this has not been the only way to increase the state's income at the expense of the deceased. VAT on funeral services and funeral goods was increased from 5% to 18% at the same time and then to a further 20%. So it's still a very bad thing, the funeral got a lot more expensive. I am not tired of repeating that Estonia has joined the European Social Charter and the Social Security Code. Funeral allowance is one of the types of social security that Estonia has considered to be binding and has ratified at the time. As we are very well aware, we have been accused of all the time, even if the funeral allowance was 3000 kroons, that it is too small. But we lost it in the first place. At present, we will only pay funeral allowance to those who receive income support. But let us look around at the extent of poverty in Estonia! Many people do not qualify for it, but there are 76% of people in Estonia who receive wages below the average. Nearly half of these will receive less than EUR 400. We have 18.7%, or 278000 people, of relative poverty. These are huge numbers. In absolute poverty, 7.3%, or nearly 100,000, live. One in three unemployed lives in absolute poverty. One in three living alone lives below the poverty line. Pensions have risen a little, but the purchasing power of the pension has actually decreased. And we have certain areas of the Alatskivi, Peipsi border, Kallaste, where 40% of people live in relative poverty. So let's think! It's a lot of poverty. Many poor people don't get income support, but they're poor from poor. In any case, it is extremely unfortunate that we cannot pay a full death grant for everyone. To be honest, it's embarrassing to even talk about it. But the most embarrassing thing should be the Reform Party and the IRL government who stopped paying the coffin money five years ago. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U247	Our next item is the second reading of the Social Welfare Act initiated by the Government of the Republic, the Labour Market Services and Benefits Act and the Act amending other laws. Commission waitress Jüri Jaanson, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U248	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The draft Act amending the Social Welfare Act, the Labour Market Services and Benefits Act and other laws was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 16 June and its first reading was completed on 17 September. The deadline for tabling amendments was 3 October. No amendments were tabled by the political groups or members of the Riigikogu. After the completion of the first reading, the Committee on Social Affairs met in eight sessions to discuss the draft. A number of opinions on the draft were sent from the draft target groups. Proposals were sent by the Estonian Association of Blind People, the Estonian Association of Patients, the Estonian Association of Visually Disabled People, the Association of Suppliers of Invaru and Special Welfare Services and, in particular, the Estonian Chamber of Disabled People and the Estonian Association of People with Mobility Disabled. At its meeting on 7 October, i.e. the first time the matter was discussed, the Committee on Social Affairs was familiar with the current rehabilitation arrangements, and discussions on the substance of the amendments were opened on 9 October. The initiator of the draft proposal asked for further amendments to be considered. On 13 and 14 November, the draft was discussed with representatives of the target groups. After examining the written opinions of the discussions, the Committee on Social Affairs decided to propose amendments to the draft. There were many amendments, I would like to introduce them more important. For example, the Estonian Chamber of Disabled People and the Estonian Central Association of Visually Disabled People, as well as the Estonian Association of People with Mobility Disabled, wanted the Estonian Unemployment Insurance Fund to compensate the employer for the cost of adjusting the jobs before people started working. Unfortunately, this amendment could not be supported, as paying the employer in advance would not speed up the adjustment and would not guarantee the end-use of labour market services. During the discussions, it was proposed to supplement the Employment Services and Benefits Act with provisions which would also allow a tool to be considered to be a necessary tool for working, regardless of disability, for carrying out work, as well as for managing a disabled person's daily life. At the same time, the Social Welfare Act regulates the provision of a tool to cope with everyday life. If a tool for everyday life is available and ensures that you can also cope with work, no additional tool will be provided. However, if you need additional help to work, you can also get it through the unemployment fund. The representatives of the Estonian Association of People with reduced mobility and the Estonian Central Association of Visual Disabled People also wanted disabled people to be assisted in employment and in the performance of their duties, while they felt it necessary that this assistance be provided throughout the employment relationship. The representatives of the Ministry of Social Affairs explained to them that the current Employment Services and Benefits Act already has services in place and, in conjunction with taking into account the additional proposals, they fulfil the stated objective. For example, § 22 of the current Act provides a service to assist with an interview and work with a support person. The latter is a service for the disabled unemployed who need help and guidance from the disabled while working and receives up to 1000 hours during the first year of employment, if necessary. In addition, the Special Welfare Service Providers' Association also proposed that the deadline for taking over the obligation to pay the rehabilitation service should be reduced to 30 days. But, unfortunately, that's not possible. The draft has taken into account 40 days for this purpose, since during this period there must be a meeting between the evaluator and the client and there must also be time for the evaluator to request additional information if necessary in order to make a final decision. There was also a request for an overview and clarification of how the procedure for special care would look. Everything was explained. The Estonian Association of People with reduced mobility and the Estonian Chamber of Disabled People pointed out that without improved services provided by the local government, the objectives of the reform could not be achieved. It was explained to us that the state supports local authorities in providing social services. To this end, the European Social Fund (ESF) 2014-2020 has also been proposed. the amounts available for the year 2009. Several practical questions were raised during the discussions. For example, representative organisations proposed the development of an electronic system for providing evidence related to the work capacity reform. This coincides in some respects with the planned one-door policy, which was already on the agenda in the planning of the entire work capacity reform. Electronic solutions, of course, depend on the development of specific information systems. They gradually combine with the reform and, of course, they also become smoother to participate in the reform. Many of the issues and amendments presented in the referrals were those that can be resolved by a government regulation. Thus, for example, the Estonian Association of Visually Disabled People wishes to pay travel and accommodation costs to a person who supports a visually impaired person when he or she travels to a rehabilitation clinic with a needy person. More diapers were also wanted to be paid to people with reduced mobility. All these proposals can be resolved by government regulations. On the amendments, perhaps so much more that they were tabled in total 33. They were initially not presented, but eventually they met at 33 and all relate to the need to improve the law amending the Social Welfare Act, the Labour Market Services and Benefits Act in order to better contribute to the implementation of the labour capacity reform. For example, in the original text of the draft, a person of working age who has part-time work capacity is entitled, under the Labour Market Services and Benefits Act, only to a professional rehabilitation service if he or she needs it to take up his or her job or to maintain it. The amendment to the bill gives people a choice. If a person participates actively in the labour market, he or she is entitled to receive rehabilitation services under the Employment Services and Benefits Act. If a person does not want to be active in the labour market, he or she does not have the right to work rehabilitation, but he or she retains the right to receive social rehabilitation services that are conducive to daily life on the basis of the Social Welfare Act. By amending the law, the circle of people who can receive social rehabilitation services will be extended to those who do not want to be active in the labour market. Several declaratory amendments stress that the provision of the service must be carried out on an individual case-by-case basis. This is one of the fundamental pillars of the whole reform. The amendments also specify the rights of the Social Insurance Board. For example, if the Agency receives information that the service does not meet the needs of the person, it has the right to have a new rehabilitation plan for the person re-evaluated. The amendment also allows the Social Insurance Board to direct the person to the service of supporting everyday life or to the service of supporting work without drawing up a separate rehabilitation plan, as well as to the service of supporting living and living in the community. All the amendments listed are actually aimed at keeping people active. As regards aids, the Committee on Social Affairs supported the proposal to increase the age limit for young learners, with a limit of 90% for the reimbursement of aids, from the age of 24 to the age of 26. The amendments also specify the status of family members compared to the current version of the Social Welfare Act. Today's law refers to family members living together in the same home, the same address, and people living together in a common household. It is also specified that if a student reaches the age of 25 in the month in which he or she applies for income support, he or she will be considered 25 until the end of the month. The proposed amendments further specify the rights to use the tool, as well as the fact that, if the tool has been used up to its prescribed depreciation rate, the tool may be left free of charge to the user. Very many amendments are also purely linguistic and technical. Finally, the procedural decisions and proposals of the lead committee. The Social Affairs Committee decided at its meeting on 6 November to submit the draft for second reading on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu by 12 November, i.e. today, and proposed that the second reading be stopped. Jüri Jaanson was appointed rapporteur for the commission. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U321	Thank you very much, Mr President. Mr President, thank you very much. ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U322	Thank you for your question. Yes, indeed, the cornerstone of the work capacity reform is the assessment of working capacity. Its cornerstones are also case-by-case arrangements, as well as activity requirements. But that's it. As far as the evaluation methodology is concerned, it actually exists. At the end of November, its actual pilotage begins. A hundred people from different locations, four counties, are involved in the piloting process, and the methodology is expected to be further refined. And this work must be done before the reform begins. The start is scheduled for 1 January 2016. The justification of the methodology for assessing the employment capacity reform is now evident in the piloting process and, of course, later in its implementation. I am certainly not going to propose here the expected number of cases based on what has taken place in England. England has a slightly different system in nature. This is a system where most of the costs of the reform were passed on to the employer. We don't have that. I stress once again that our project is unique, our project is novel. I very much hope that the piloting will assure us that the methodology for assessing the ability to work is watery. That is why I do not want to manipulate or throw into the air any predicament here. I assume we've made a good start. And we will go on with this, we will continue this during the next parliamentary term, and we will implement the work capacity reform as a whole.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U363	Thank you, Mr President. Good waitress! We have a good example of this draft here, and I am very grateful to you for that. ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U364	Thank you for your question. When we talk specifically about this bill, it is in support of the draft law on capacity support for work, which will be discussed here next. Basically, there are three things that this bill will change. First, a uniform administration of aids, second, the organisation of rehabilitation services. Rehabilitation services become social and work-based, professional. Social rehabilitation, as is still the case with the Social Insurance Institution, is dealt with by occupational rehabilitation, i.e. rehabilitation, which helps people to get a job and work, is dealt with by the unemployment fund. Labour market services, such as the possibility of working with a support person and getting help for an interview, will also be added. These are services that help a person with partial work capacity to first cope with the activity requirements of the unemployment fund, secondly, help him to get a job, and thirdly, help him to endure at work. These services are intended to support both the needy and the employer. These are the three main points that the Social Welfare Act and the Labour Market Support and Services Act should improve.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U373	Thank you for your question. One of the main changes is the biggest minus of the current system. It has been said from year to year that the current system will provide an average of 483 euros per person for rehabilitation. The same draft, which is being dealt with, divides rehabilitation into two parts: one is social rehabilitation and the other is occupational rehabilitation, which is then provided by the Social Insurance Institution and the unemployment fund respectively. Another very important change is the higher amount which, according to current calculations, can be allocated to one person's rehabilitation of EUR 1 500 per person. So, in financial terms, the difference is huge. Plus, in certain specific cases, especially social rehabilitation organised by the Social Insurance Board, there is no longer any need for a specific rehabilitation plan. A person is sent directly to use the service. These are the main changes that should improve access to rehabilitation.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U376	Thank you. I now request the speech chair of the Riigikogu, Margus Tsahkna! Just as it's customary, if you want, you can have three more minutes. Would you like some? Please, eight minutes!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U377	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Dear members of the Riigikogu, as well as people who follow this debate online! We actually have two drafts in the process. One is what is currently under discussion, i.e. a draft law amending the Social Welfare Act, the Labour Market Services and Benefits Act and other laws. Maybe a lot of people don't understand what's behind this title. If we look at the whole issue of work capacity reform, it is precisely in this bill that there should be answers to all the questions that people with disabilities and organisations with special needs have asked. The work capacity reform that will be discussed in this Chamber as the next draft should be based on the draft that we are currently discussing. If we look at the role models of the work capacity reform, the Ministry has, for example, introduced 'practical systems from other countries', the Netherlands, Australia, Great Britain and some countries closer to us have adopted the same ideology. But it has been forgotten one 'pisiasi', namely that there is a functioning social welfare system in all these countries. All these countries have created prerequisites for the implementation of the labour capacity reform. So, what are these assumptions? These prerequisites are the resolution of obvious problems. These are all the problems that people with disabilities have referred to, and which are actually put together in one word: it is the fact that people with disabilities cannot get out of their homes. The current situation is that the reform of working capacity places the obligation on persons with disabilities to be active, to be included in the labour market, to be involved in active labour market measures. The disabled have not objected to this, and no party in this Chamber has argued against it either. The current social system has basically assigned people with disabilities to their homes. There has not been much support from the state so that they can move so that they can participate actively in society. Now we are putting this responsibility on them through the work capacity reform, and I think they might even be happy to be interested in them and to be able to go to work according to their abilities, to get out of the house. The same draft, which we are discussing now, does propose improvements to the current system, specifies the system of sharing aids and rehabilitation issues, but these services, which should be provided primarily by local authorities, are not listed here. The main proposals of disabled people were that we, at the level of the Riigikogu, lay down minimum standards for specific social welfare measures by law. The state has delegated the provision of social welfare services to municipalities. This regulation is general and, as we know, the possibilities and perhaps also the will of local governments are very different across Estonia. I think that these services are better accessible to people in Tallinn, Tartu and other major cities. With the work capacity reform, we impose on disabled people the obligation to actively use labour market measures, but across Estonia there are unsolicited opportunities for them to get out of their homes to get even into the unemployment fund for evaluation or work if they really have a job, for example, if an employer has created a job for them. That is why I regret that the draft is before us in this form. Indeed, I must admit that the Committee on Social Affairs has tried to change this, and these changes are also before you, but the basic problem is not solved by the draft law amending the Social Welfare Act and the Labour Market Services and Benefits Act. This key problem is the lack of access to these same services. The second point I want to make briefly is that the reform of working capacity imposes an additional obligation on people with special needs. He will be evaluated to obtain the information he needs to be rehabilitated in order to help him participate in society in order to help him work. At the same time, this draft, which we are discussing here today, will remain a separate system for assigning disability to the Social Insurance Board and for the rehabilitation organisation, which has so far been under very great criticism, no major changes will be made. This system will remain so-called social rehabilitation and occupational rehabilitation will be separate. One positive change is that the system of aids will be centralized nationally on a regional basis. This is a welcome step, but the whole system remains separate. Broadly speaking, it is quite difficult to understand whether the draft writers have been able to put themselves in the situation of the disabled person who has to go through the whole system in order to be able to respond to future active labour market measures so that he can receive support. I specifically mention the target of 15 000 new jobs to be created by 2020. 15,000 people with special needs should be able to work. At the same time, incentives for employers do not change significantly; they remain more or less the same as they are today. It is not certain that businesses will create more jobs, that they will be more interested in people with disabilities in the future, but that the obligation to act is imposed on people with disabilities. Finally, I would like to say that the Group of the Fatherland and Res Publica Alliance also proposes to interrupt the second reading of the Social Welfare Act, the Labour Market Services and Benefits Act and the Law amending other laws. We do not want to send a message that we are fundamentally opposed to this reform. We are not against this reform; we are in favour of achieving these objectives with two hands. It is precisely this law that could, in a better way, create the prerequisites for people with disabilities and people with special needs to be able to fulfil their obligations under the capacity for work reform. I call for common sense to prevail and for the second reading to be interrupted! Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U379	Let's move on to item eight. The second reading of the draft Employment Capacity Allowance Act 678 initiated by the Government of the Republic begins. Jüri Jaanson, Member of the Social Affairs Committee of the Riigikogu Chair, please present!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U380	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Draft Employment Allowance Act 678 was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 2 June. and the first reading was completed on 18 June. At that time, the deadline for submitting amendments was 11 August. Within the deadline, 16 amendments were tabled, eight of which were tabled by the Centre Party Group and eight were proposed by the members of the Riigikogu Heljo Pikhof, Rein Aidma, Karel Rüütel and here. After the first reading of the draft Act, the Social Affairs Committee discussed the draft Employment Capacity Allowance Act at a total of 15 sessions, essentially throughout the summer and autumn. All the parties and target groups were involved in the discussions of the draft Employment Capacity Support Act, apart from the Estonian Association of Judges, Public Prosecutor's Office, Estonian Taxpayers' Association, Estonian Unemployment Insurance Fund, Estonian Patients' Association, as well as the Estonian Trade Union Confederation. A public address to the Government of the Republic, the Riigikogu and the public of Estonia has also been submitted on the draft. In addition, the Estonian Chamber of Disabled People and the Estonian Trade Union Confederation sent a joint return. The Institute of Social Work at Tallinn University also sent an overview of the results of an international survey on the employment of people with mental disorders. I will take a closer look at the procedure. In the summer, on 15 and 16 July, meetings were held with interest groups. The Social Affairs Committee also examined the results of the studies and analyses of the Centre for Policy Studies in Praxis. Representatives of the Estonian Reuma Union, the Estonian Society of Multiplex Associations and the Estonian Association of People with Disabilities of Mobility took part in the meeting and presented their problems. Meetings were also held with representatives of the Estonian Chamber of Disabled People and employers. A month later, after the deadline for amendments fell on 25 August, the Committee on Social Affairs examined the current methodology for assessing incapacity for work and the changes to be made in the future. The Social Committee also looked at the planned methodology for assessing the ability to work. On 26 August, the meetings held with the complainants continued. All the meetings were attended as representatives of the initiators by the officials of the Ministry of Social Affairs, who provided further explanations and answered questions from the members of the Commission. Let me talk a little bit about the discussions with the parties and the proposals that have been put forward. One very important thing was that the interest groups wanted to raise the income limit, starting with a reduction in the capacity allowance for workers with partial or lack of working capacity. The Social Affairs Committee agreed with the interest groups and supported the proposal by both the Estonian Centre Party and the members of the aforementioned Riigikogu to raise the income limit to 30 days. The aid will therefore start to be reduced from EUR 960 per salary and the partial capacity to work will cease to be paid from EUR 1325 per salary at the current daily rate. I would point out that, according to the current daily rate, in the future, this amount will always be increased. A person with a lack of working capacity shall cease to receive the allowance for a salary of EUR 1600. Discussions also brought out the very strong desire of stakeholders to introduce a "one-door" system, so that all operations can be carried out in one institution, such as a declaration to assess working capacity and receive work capacity support, as well as the identification of disability and the determination of its severity. The principle is that the unemployment fund and the Social Insurance Board exchange data and people do not run from one place to another for no reason. In principle, this change was introduced: if a person turns to an unemployment fund to assess his or her working capacity, he or she can also apply for a disability check there and is already delivered to the Social Insurance Board by the unemployment fund. Stakeholders expressed concern that compliance with the activity requirements for disability benefit would lead to the running of disabled people and would be too difficult for them, as disabled people would not be able to move easily. The representatives of the Ministry of Social Affairs and the unemployment fund confirmed that activity requirements can, inter alia, be met by telephone or electronic means. In addition, in order to alleviate the fears of stakeholders, the Social Committee decided to propose an amendment emphasising the role of the unemployment fund in the administrative procedure, i.e. the unemployment fund is obliged in every case where there is a risk of loss of work capacity benefit, to identify the circumstances in which a person does not comply with the activity requirements. The unemployment fund must certainly not be suspended until the circumstances have been clarified. The Estonian Chamber of Disabled People and the Estonian Trade Union Confederation pointed out that according to the draft, a person must have attended a doctor's appointment in the previous three months to assess his working ability. The Committee on Social Affairs agreed that this time was too short for a disabled person due to long queues with a doctor, a lack of mobility of a disabled person or a lack of ability to move around and other objective circumstances. Therefore, on the proposal of the members of the Riigikogu Heljo Pikhof, Rein Aidma, Karel Rüütli and Jüri Jaanson, this period was extended to six months. Discussions with the participation of many different stakeholders raised the question of how the assessment of working capacity will be carried out on the basis of a new methodology. I'm going to explain this right here. The first step is that a person goes to a family doctor or a specialist who describes his/her medical condition and inserts data into the health information system. The person must then submit an application to the unemployment fund for an assessment of his or her capacity to work. It shall complete a questionnaire on its operational capacity and shall initially assess its own capacity. If necessary, the case manager of the unemployment fund will interview him to complete the questionnaire. At the same time, I would stress that an application for a disability allowance can also be submitted, as it is the date on which the application is submitted that will be the date on which the capacity allowance will be paid. As a third step, the assessment of the ability to work is carried out by the health care provider. As a fourth step, a person gets a decision to assess his or her ability to work; he or she can have a full, partial or missing capacity. The decision is made by a specialist on the basis of the expert opinion of the health care provider on the assessment of the working capacity of the unemployment fund. These four steps need to be taken by a person, and his or her ability to work is then appreciated. However, as I pointed out in the debate on the draft law, the methodology for assessing the ability to work is still being tested in order to correct the details. After evaluation, the person is further dealt with by the case manager of the unemployment fund, who advises, coordinates and evaluates the person's need for employment services and rehabilitation. Criticism about the system of performance assessment has been done by the Estonian Association of Persons with reduced mobility. They consider that it is not justified that there are uniform grounds for assessing the ability of persons with disabilities and persons with illness to work; they consider that they should be different. But the Ministry of Social Affairs did not consider it justified to assess the working capacity of disabled and disabled persons on different grounds. When applying for an assessment of the ability to work, a person can make an individual assessment, but the general basis of the assessment must be uniform. Out of the proposals made by the stakeholders, compensation for the additional costs of assessing the ability to work was also included in the draft. If a person has to go to an expert doctor's appointment, he/she will therefore incur additional costs, such as the use of special transport, personal assistant or sign language interpreter, which will be reimbursed if the valuer of the unemployment fund is unable to assess the person himself and sends him/her to the health care provider. During the discussions, representatives of the Ministry of Social Affairs and the unemployment fund introduced an employment programme for people with reduced working capacity and their employers, which will provide the target group with already existing labour market services, as well as completely new services, the details of which are currently being dealt with in the preparation for formulation and implementation. I will also talk a little bit about the amendments. The one-door system has already been discussed. The amendments focus on what has been said. Amendment 30 adds a letter to the means of service of the decision of the unemployment fund. It provides an opportunity to send administrative decisions to people other than other documents. Very many target groups have taken an interest in this. Amendment 32, initiated by the aforementioned members of the Riigikogu, stresses once again the importance of identifying the circumstances when the payment of the capacity allowance is suspended. In particular, according to the draft Employment and Health Insurance Act, the unemployment fund has the right to suspend the payment of disability benefit for 10 days if the beneficiary does not come to the unemployment fund for the first time without a valid reason, refuses to comply with the job search plan or does not accept a suitable job. The unemployment fund has the right to stop paying the disability allowance if the person who is registered as unemployed with partial employment capacity ceases to be registered or does not come to the unemployment fund for at least a third time without good reason at the time he or she is assigned. It is very important to stress that the amendment requires the unemployment fund to clarify the circumstances of each particular case and that it is only for a very good reason that it is possible to withdraw the disability allowance. Some of the amendments delayed the implementation of the employment capacity reform by half a year, until 1 January 2016. There was also much talk about employing thousands of people in the public sector, with which the public sector would set an example for the private sector. Part of the amendments included linguistic and regulatory changes. I will now pass on the procedural decisions and proposals of the commission. The Social Affairs Committee decided at its meeting on 6 November to send the draft to second reading in the Riigikogu plenary session by 12 November and proposed that the second reading be completed. The draft was still on the agenda of the Social Committee on 10 November when the second reading procedural documents were approved. Jüri Jaanson, member of the Social Affairs Committee, was appointed rapporteur for the commission. It was a brief review. Thank you.

The answer was yes, we would, because we have nowhere to go. But the other part of the question was, what kind of money would we do that with? I'd say it's coming out of today's subject. There is no point in asking hypothetical questions right now. We have European money to reform the system of disability pensions and we can use it. However, when we talk about finding money from the state budget, it is, after all, a matter of discussion of the state budget. This issue should be discussed when the state budget is being made, not in the context of the labour capacity reform.

It is a question of equal opportunities for people with partial employment capacity to apply for jobs in the open labour market, whether in the public or private sector. From an economic point of view, we are also not talking about job creation, but rather about the fact that the number of people in employment in the near future will quite certainly decrease, as the number of inhabitants will decrease. It also forces employers to select and search for employees better. I also see a motivation here to help employers bring people with partial work capacity closer together.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U499	Thank you for your question. First of all, the decision to suspend payment is taken in two levels, as has been explained to us. First of all, the case manager fixes the facts. Secondly, the case manager must certainly come to an understanding of why this has happened, why these reasons have occurred. If there are no valid reasons, the special Council of the unemployment fund will assess the circumstances and decide whether or not to suspend the payment of the disability allowance. The question, however, is, above all, whether a person really had good reason to break the rules, if he did not come to the unemployment fund in time, did not accept the job offered, or did not comply with the requirements of activity in any other way. In addition, it can be said that this process is personal, a decision is made personally on the basis of these reasons. This is different from the rules on ordinary people, as they automatically lose unemployment benefit because of their non-compliance with the rules.

There are a series of activities that would have been and could have been done for disabled people within the framework of the laws already in force, but for some reason either are not wanted, able or not able to do. Let me give you a few examples that Pille Ilves, the head of the Estonian Patients' Association, has given us. He draws attention to the OECD report 'Decreasing poverty in Estonia through activation and better targeting', which contains a number of tables about Estonia that at the same time startle and sadden. It is clear from the study of the report that Estonia is extremely stingy in supporting its people compared to other countries. Estonia's share of social protection expenditure in GDP is lower than in any other OECD country, with the exception of Korea and Mexico. We can see that only 50% of the unemployed receive unemployment benefit, which amounts to only one third of the total amount corresponding to the absolute poverty line, and that support for the poor accounts for 0.1% of GDP, almost invisible compared to others, as the OECD average is 2%. In addition, Estonia is one of the few countries in Europe where uninsured persons do not receive medical care and where long-term unemployment is punished by the loss of medical care. It should be added that the recent reduction in sickness benefits has caused people to work sick and only 6% of those who are ill take the sick list. This information leads to only one logical conclusion: Estonia itself, with its austerity policy, has created a large number of pensioners for incapacity for work and is directly responsible for the deterioration of the health of its people. The OECD is surprised that Estonia, where the share of social benefits in GDP is so low, spends a large part of it on the most generous parental benefit in Europe. To sum up, once again, we must say that we are trying to tackle the problem that we ourselves have created with the reform. Worse still, we will continue to produce the people on whom half and unfinished reform will be tested. In particular, the state should create preventative conditions so that more and more people do not become the subject of this so-called labour reform. There is no understanding of why people with disabilities do not want to work and this involuntaryity must be eliminated by coercive measures. A person who feels the situation knows that the real causes of disability problems are much deeper. The most important of these are the lack of effective rehabilitation and the availability of life support services and tools. Reformers have missed important questions about what happens to their own carers and where jobs come from. Activative measures without jobs and a functioning service system will never work properly and can produce a number of undesirable results. The duty of activity, which does not actually have outputs, does not encourage going to work, but adds meaningless burdens to families, which are already under pressure. It could also be added that the National Audit Office has had some of the same views on this reform in the letter sent to us. So I would like to ask why we are doing reform when those for whom we are supposed to do so are not in any way willing to accept it and do not want to adopt it. They are ready to come to the Riigikogu, half sick in autumn cold weather, in a wheelchair, to ask once again that we do not adopt these laws in this form. But really, aren't we here in particular I would like to stress that this has been done by the coalition, I think, by practicing hard how not to listen to the will of the people. I dare remind you of the law that was passed some time ago, on which according to the survey two thirds of the Estonian people were opposed, but the law was still passed. I believe that this two-thirds of the people will show their attitude in the forthcoming elections. Thank you.

In order for him to be able to do this so that he does not lose his job and can reconcile family and working life, parental benefit is a very good measure. It is well - favored that a young parent does not lose his or her qualifications. He's actually a healthy, good man, young, strong, and full of work. The parental benefit only compensates for the time it takes to raise a child in addition to working, and can also be used for one hour per working day, for example. Indeed, this is a work capacity allowance to enable reconciliation between family and work. If we already have a problem with young healthy women who are with one small disability, namely, they are of childbearing age, it is difficult to find a job, let alone have a child or, worse, children, we will see that we have not been able to solve this issue even in such a self-evident situation, unlike, for example, the Nordic countries, which have done much better with it. If we look at the reform that is currently under discussion and compare it with the logic of parental benefit, we can see that these two logics run against each other cross: in one case they are completely healthy people who have been decided to pay 100% of the allowance and assume that they are either not going to work at all or going to work a little bit, only as little as the state allows, and in the other case we are trying to do the opposite. In my opinion, these logics do not coincide. I now come to this source of inspiration, the Pay Information Agency and the labour market survey of partners, including alma mater. ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U537	Ladies and gentlemen, Labour capacity reform has seriously affected Estonian society. People's ambassadors have received many letters and messages calling for improvements to the draft laws. Why? Many critics believe that the reason and effect of the current work capacity reform plan is mixed and based on sensitive assumptions. The tools of the reform are the introduction of a commitment to activity and the creation of a new evaluation system which, given the current plan, will not help to achieve the reform objectives even if the best will is desired. The sustainability of the Estonian social security system is not threatened by the increase in the number of people with reduced working capacity, but by the ageing population. The advocates of the reform have argued that without the reform, the Estonian social protection system would collapse, because people with disabilities are constantly gaining weight and this is beyond the control of the state's purse. The explanatory memorandum to the bill contains a table showing the growth of the last six years and giving the impression that this growth will not stop and it will be bad. At the same time, however, the figure drawn up by Praxis analysts has not been included in the explanatory memorandum, which shows a very different trend until 2057. The number of pensioners increased only during the recession, and it has stopped now. The number of pensioners in incapacity for work is constantly decreasing until 2057. The problem of the social security system is, on the contrary, the ageing of the population, so the argument justifying the reform is wrong, as the reform of working capacity cannot stop the ageing of the population in any way. The reform is based on the assumption that the number of pensioners in incapacity for work has increased because they are deceiving the system and do not want to go to work. This assumption is deeply mistaken and shows ignorance. On the actual situation of the Estonian social system, the OECD report 'Decreasing poverty in Estonia through activation and better targeting' contains a number of tables that simultaneously startle and sadden. It is clear from the study of the report that Estonia is extremely stingy in supporting its people compared to other countries. The share of Estonia's social protection expenditure in GDP is lower than in any other OECD country, with the exception of Korea and Mexico. This concerns the report on social policy. A number of tables illustrate Estonia's cheapening at the expense of its people. We can see that only 50% of the unemployed receive unemployment benefit, which amounts to only one third of the amount corresponding to the absolute poverty line, and that the expenditure on support for the poor represents 0.1% of GDP, almost invisible compared to other countries' expenditure, as the OECD countries average is 2%. We also see that Estonia is among the latter in terms of health indicators and that the poor population has a higher indicator of poor health than in other countries. In addition, Estonia is one of the few countries in Europe where uninsured persons do not receive medical care and long-term unemployment is punished by the loss of medical care. It should be added that the recent reduction in sickness benefits has caused people to work sick and only 6% of those who are ill take the sick list. This information leads to only one logical conclusion: Estonia itself, with its austerity policy, has created a large number of pensioners for incapacity for work and is directly responsible for the deterioration of the health of its people. The OECD is surprised that in Estonia, where the share of social benefits in GDP is so low, a large part of it is spent on untargeted and Europe's most generous parental benefits, although research suggests that in the long term the creation of an appropriate growth environment for children will increase ivet. The principle is highlighted: more for those in need. At the same time, the OECD is satisfied with the targeting of disability benefits, saying that although the benefits are small, they are rather well targeted at the poorer population. Also, against the background of otherwise poor results, the rate of high-employment of disabled people is positive, in which Estonia is the honourable third most. It turns out that disability pensions are not the enemy of work. More than half of disabled people are already in work and do so without any coercion. However, the current reform aims at disability pensions and the employment of disabled people, which makes it difficult to be satisfied. Thank you!

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U547	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, Over the last few hours, we have been able to actively discuss once again the pillars of the work capacity reform: how to approach this reform, why to approach it and whether to do so at all, whether it is right to interrupt it or what to do with it. Several problems have been highlighted and many criticisms have been made. I listened to all of this with interest. Some of the criticisms are justified. Work capacity reform is diverse. There is no doubt that the reform of working capacity must become the starting point for a very large and broad undertaking involving people with reduced working capacity, including people with disabilities, in society. However, I certainly cannot accept the arguments put forward here and sent to all of us in writing, as if the reform was based on the assumption that the current system is being deceived. Without knowing the basic data, we cannot oppose or agree that by 2057 we will not have an increase in the number of people with reduced working capacity. On the contrary, other studies show that this figure will increase and increase significantly by 2023. These are alternative studies. Costs will increase from EUR 250 million to EUR 437 million by 2023. I would like to remind you once again that both the National Audit Office and many other institutions have said that the current system needs to be restructured and this should be done even if we do not have the opportunity to receive European money as support. I recall a few important positive things that the work capacity reform should bring with it. One of the most important is a flexible individual approach to each individual case. Secondly, the amendments to the Social Welfare Act and the Labour Market Services and Benefits Act, intended as supporting laws, will undoubtedly provide all the necessary assistance for a person of working age with health impairment in order to remain able to work and achieve this ability. In summing up, I would like to emphasise once again that the work capacity reform can be seen as a broad exercise, here you can look for mistakes, it can be linked to the economy and education. It's all right. I fully agree with the rapporteur who said that one of the key issues in getting people with reduced working capacity to work is raising the level of education. But I want to say once again that somewhere this is the starting point, if we have to tell the system that that's enough and we're going to continue in a new way. The new system is completely novel and the new system always carries some risks, so it is important to test the details. I am convinced that it is necessary to implement this reform now and now. With these draft laws... I'm asking for more time!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U548	Three more minutes.
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U549	... progress is necessary. I urge you all to think that this is rather a positive change and that it will help people to be more involved. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U562	Thank you! The first reading of the draft Fisheries Market Organisation Act 744 took place in this Chamber on 22 October. This draft provides the basis for the implementation of the EMFF funds for the financial period 2014 to 2020. It regulates the granting of State aid and de minimis aid and lays down the principles of the single market organisation resulting from European Union regulations. The deadline for submitting amendments was set at 31 October. By this time the members of the Riigikogu, the political groups and other committees did not propose amendments. The following procedural decisions were taken at the meeting of the Commission on 4 and 10 November: it was decided to put a draft of 744 on the agenda for the plenary session by today, 12 November, to propose that the second reading of the draft be stopped, and when the second reading is completed, it is proposed that the draft be sent to third reading by 19 November. ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U588	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The Estonian farmer is currently competing not with another farmer in Estonia, but primarily with other farmers in the European Union. In order for competition to be fair and possible, the support of the Estonian Government to our farmers must be equivalent to that of other countries. Today we are discussing the draft law implementing the European Union's common agricultural policy and we see that the Estonian state is allowed to provide additional support to its farmers by means of transitional payments. Their aim is to reduce the income gap between Estonian farmers compared to producers in other European Union Member States and thereby help our farmers to be competitive. In other words, with the aid of transitional payments, we can compensate our farmers for the smaller direct payments from the European Union compared to those paid to producers in the old Member States. Today, however, we are dealing with a bill that is already known that the Clothing Government is not going to comply with it. Minister of Agriculture Ivari Padar has said that he too does not see opportunities in the 2015 state budget to support our own Estonian farmers with 23 million euros. At the same time, other Baltic countries have promised to support their farmers with transitional support. If the current government intends to pursue such an agricultural policy, the Estonian farmer will not be able to compete even on the Latvian and Lithuanian markets, let alone on the markets of the wealthier Member States of the European Union. The Estonian government could learn from the Latvian and Lithuanian governments how to protect their farmers, producers of food in their own country. According to the European Union's common agricultural policy, Estonia will be able to pay transitional payments until 2020. The Centre Party is of the opinion that the Estonian state must seize this opportunity and find the 23 million euros needed from the state budget already in 2015. Let it be said here that the Central Party Group has proposed to find additional money for farmers, and we have also foreseen sources of cover for this purpose. EUR 23 million of additional support would be very important money for rural enterprises, as farmers now have to cope with losses caused by the decline in markets, apart from shrinking subsidies. Farmers are disappointed. They have acknowledged that in the current market situation, State support has become even more important than the new markets that the state is looking for, but has not yet actually found. The previous Prime Minister did not favour the agricultural sector but inhibited its activities, while the food industry is a direct security guarantee for each country, which is why the state should ensure normal food production conditions. The current Prime Minister has not brought new energy to the agricultural sector, but rather continues his predecessor's policy of deploring agriculture and less important to the sector than to other sectors. Estonian agriculture has been hit especially in recent months by one concern after another. It is therefore particularly strange to hear from the Clothing Government that farmers can only be supported at the expense of children's benefits. This completely unjustified contrast is unethical. The law on the implementation of the common agricultural policy of the European Union on the table enables our farmers to enjoy equal conditions of competition in the common market of the European Union. This opportunity should also be used by the Estonian Government. The Centre Party is certainly not in favour of the agricultural policy of the government in office, which puts farmers in a more inferior position on the European Union market than competitors. We are doing everything to ensure that the Estonian state supports agriculture and that Estonian farmers can compete equally on the European Union market. Thank you.

If we look at the two pillars together, which together is about EUR 2 billion, we will see that with this amount we will ultimately not be among the least supported countries in the European Union. I mean, these two support must be looked at together. Of course, our decision is precisely how much money we move from one pillar to another. ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U626	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! With the entry into force of the Civil Service Act last year, the Minister of Finance received the task of giving the Riigikogu an overview of the development of the public service once a year. Today's introduction is the first of its kind. The public service covers public authorities and local authorities, which are significantly smaller than the government sector or the public sector. In 2013, 299550 people were employed in the public service, i.e. 22% of all employees in the public sector. Compared to 2008, when the number of civil servants peaked, the number of people employed in the public service has fallen by nearly 6%. Of those in the public service, more than 24000 people worked in public institutions and 5,733 in local government institutions. There were 21,500 officials in the public service and 8500 employees with less than 8500 contracts of employment. The entry into force of the new law led to a 21% reduction in the number of officials, as the concept of an official was severely narrowed. However, general attention is usually paid to employees of the central government of the public sector, as well as to the government sector, but there is a clear distinction to be made between sectors. Officials include, for example, police officers and public sector teachers and doctors. Let me tell you a little bit more about the government sector. The number of officials decreased last year, but the number of employees in the central government increased by 1% as the number of employees in foundations and public institutions increased by 6.4%. At the same time, the number of employees in state budget institutions decreased by 2%. This means that the state has managed to control institutions closer to the so-called central government, but those that go further are expanding. This process must certainly be given more attention in the future. The working-age population of Estonia is declining and it is natural to expect that the number of employees of the general government will also change more or less in sync with this. It is important that the share of employees in the general government does not increase. This means that the number of these workers should fall by around 500 every year, perhaps even faster. Is such a dynamic possible? Yeah, but that means a lot of work. The number of employees must not be reduced in such a way that we simply move important functions of public authority to other parts of the government sector. Things need to be viewed in a complex and general way. It is important to ensure that the functions of public authority remain where they belong. Professional and dedicated officials are one of the foundations of the country. It is therefore important that such workers find a suitable place within the public service rather than leaving it. Various developments can be seen here. The first relates to remuneration. Last year, the average monthly salary for officials was EUR 1181, which is 25% above the national average. A higher level is justified by higher than average qualifications for officials, and the responsibility of officials is often much higher. The proportion of officials with higher education was 71% last year, while the economy as a whole had 39% employees with higher education. Last year, civil servants' wages increased faster than average. When we look at real wages, public officials grew by 10%, but on average 4% in the country. However, it must not be blamed that the increase in civil servants' salaries is too rapid. The economic crisis in the government sector meant a much greater decline in real wages than in the economy. 2009-2012. In 2008, the real wage decreased by 16% in the general government, but by 4% in the economy as a whole. After this strong growth last year, we need to ensure that wage growth does not turn out to be too strong in the future. However, this does not mean that in individual areas of the public sector, faster wage increases are not justified. The state budget for the coming year has thus planned a higher wage increase for teachers, doctors, ambulance and care providers, cultural workers and internal security staff. An important aspect of the development of the official body is certainly the survival of people at work and the turnover of the workforce. The severe wage decline during and after the crisis slightly increased the turnover of workers, but it has slightly decreased as a result of wage increases. It was 7% last year in public institutions. The new Civil Service Act made it mandatory to find officials through the organisation of a competition. It should be noted that competitions have generally succeeded. 13 applications were received last year on average. The share of lost competitions decreased from 19.5% to 15.5%, but this figure could be better. Finding top executives for public service is a cause for concern. Recently, the number of candidates in the competitions has been modest. Just as the private sector and the public sector in general need to take into account that managers do not have the most important aspect of remuneration for continuing or accepting work, it is more important to have suitable tasks and to be able to achieve results. This means that the quality of governance and development opportunities must also be addressed more seriously in the public service and in the wider government sector. These aspects are also important at lower levels of service. In order to obtain and maintain good employees, career planning, new tasks and self-development must be carried out in the public service. This issue has been addressed, but what we have achieved cannot be quite satisfied with. A White Paper on the human resources policy of the state as an employer has been completed in order to meet all the above tasks, as well as other challenges and developments. It describes the objectives of national human resources policy and provides appropriate solutions to achieve them. This book will be submitted by the Ministry of Finance to the government for approval in the near future. Thank you for listening.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U669	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! The Estonian economy grew by 1.6% in 2013. Although this was less than forecast, this figure was higher than the European Union average. The slower-than-expected economic growth was caused by a weaker position than the expectations of foreign markets, especially in the countries that are important to Estonia in Finland and Sweden. Despite the poorer growth forecast, labour market developments were positive. The number of people occupied increased to 621300, which is 1% more than a year earlier. Unemployment fell to 8.6% and average wage growth reached 7%. Last year Estonia continued a strict fiscal policy, thus ensuring the long-term sustainability of public finances. The government budget deficit was forecast to be 0.7% of GDP by the end of 2013 in the National Budget Strategy for the years 2013-2016. In practice, a better result was achieved and, according to the data reviewed, 0.5% of GDP was achieved, while 0.2% of older GDP was achieved on the basis of a time series. The reduction in the deficit was offset by higher tax revenues and lower costs than projected. 2013 was the first year in which the state increased social benefits, including pensions, child benefits, unemployment benefits, as well as public wages. Alongside a number of positive developments, negative growth and a reduction in the number of children born continue to be a cause for concern. In 2013, the next one was launched in 2014‐2020. planning the use of structural support for the 2010 budget period. Now let me give you a little more details about the consolidated financial report. The summary report for the financial year shall reflect the accounts of each budgetary authority. In addition, the report provides data on the whole public sector and government sector, as well as on local authorities. Unlike in the past, a report on the implementation of the state budget has been published, where the budget and its implementation can be compared from a series of lines. As a result, the transparency of the report has increased, but the control of expenditure has also been simplified. For the first time, the Board issued an opinion on the achievement of the objective of the structural balance of the general government. The Council estimates that the structural surplus of the general government in 2013 was in line with expectations, while the Council considers that the medium-term objectives of the country's fiscal strategy in recent years and their changes are not sufficiently justified. The state's annual accounts consist of a balance sheet, a profit and loss account, a cash flow statement, a statement of changes in net assets and a report on the implementation of the state budget. I'll tell you a little bit more about them. The 2013 State's revenue was EUR 284.6 million, or 35% more than in 2012. Despite an increase of 4.9% in revenue, with tax revenues of 6,5%, the public sector was able to maintain a moderate increase in operating costs of 3.9%. At the same time, government revenue decreased in 2013 compared to 2012, as government expenditure increased more than revenue growth, and local government expenditure increased faster than revenue. If we look at the public sector as a whole, it has to be said that the operating result remained at the level of the previous year, but the overall result fell to EUR 581 million due to financial revenues. The volume of state assets increased by 6.8% over the year to EUR 12.5 billion. The value of fixed assets increased by 9,2%, as did all government and public assets, including fixed assets. Looking at commitments, the public sector's liabilities amounted to nearly EUR 5.5 billion at the end of the year, or 29.7% of GDP, consisting of long-term liabilities, of which loans issued by the EFSF represent a significant part. They were also the reason for the increase in debt liabilities. General government liabilities increased to EUR 4.2 billion, which is 22.9% of GDP, including local governments to EUR 1.1 billion, which is 5.9% of GDP. The increase in net assets over the year, including the increase in reserves in the public sector to EUR 502.5 million, was significant. The general government reserves amounted to EUR 621.1 million and the public sector to more than EUR 1 billion. According to the audit report submitted by the National Audit Office, the state's annual accounts for 2013 were substantially correct and the main economic transactions of the state were carried out in accordance with the State Budget Act. The National Audit Office also pointed to positive changes. For example, the structure of the state budget makes it possible to compare the budget and its implementation better. The centralisation of accounting and the use of integrated accounting software have also improved the quality of accounting in most ministries. Honourable Riigikogu! Estonia has been able to further develop its economy despite the worse conditions in our neighbourhood. This has also been possible because we have been guided by the principle of conservative fiscal policy. Continuing in the chosen direction, we will be able to keep Estonia's economy on a positive path in the future. Thank you for your attention.

Attention was paid to foundations, as the state's participation in them is not always clearly targeted and the rules for allocating funds to foundations need to be clarified. ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U697	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! A year has passed and I am again in front of you with the annual report of the National Audit Office, a report that gives an overview of the problems that have been generalised as a result of our state accounts, public finances and other audits. Yeah, it's not just about the sentence, "It's okay, and there's a carefree future ahead of us. ' That is not what the National Audit Office's review will say, not now or probably in the future. This is due to the universal role of the supreme audit institutions in the state system and to the peculiar nature of our activities. We represent an impartial and independent view and, above all, our task is to bring to decision-makers the problems that need to be resolved. Nevertheless, in order to be balanced, I will start with things that are good. Public accounts are good, public finances are good, tax revenue is good for the state. The figures show that people have more work − it is a good thing. People buy more things, too, it's good. Prices are rising slowly or not at all, and this is good for those who buy things. But this picture isn't complete. If you look at these issues more closely, we have to admit that the picture is getting more complicated. Ultimately, the state's accounting and accompanying reporting are in terms of figures, but what we know about what we have done for money is little or only what we are shown. We could be sure of the trend if growth in jobs and wages were to come from smart, export-oriented companies with above average added value, and innovative working arrangements would ensure Europe's best productivity. With added value and innovation, things have not moved much forward in Estonia recently. The new Minister of Finance of the Government also wrote in his blog six months ago a longer piece of the outlook for the Estonian economy and stated with concern: "The economic outlook in Estonia in the near future is poor, there are several reasons for this. ' I have no reason to doubt the Minister's words that the prospects are poor or that there are many reasons. Against the background of the above, it is important to acknowledge that our spending must be wise, one that will make the state better for all people in Estonia. The control of the utilisation and preservation of state assets has long since ceased to be the recounting of ducats, kroons or euros in the Treasury. The task of the National Audit Office is primarily to bring out the buts and risks of governance honestly. Many may not like it, but liking or dislike cannot be a criterion for the quality of the reports or work of the National Audit Office. That is why I am telling you that the pace of economic development in Estonia has slowed down or stopped, and indecisiveness hinders our development. Understandably, it is very difficult to choose a reference base for assessing what is happening in society. Therefore, the National Audit Office tries to make as few choices as possible outside the criteria set by the government for itself. When we talk about Estonia not getting much closer to the average European figure in 2013, we mean the concept of convergence with Europe, which the government has written down in the Competitiveness Programme 'Estonia 2020'. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! Let us not forget that the most important thing is neither the ranking nor the comparison with others, be it countries like us, Latvia and Lithuania. What matters is what our current situation is and whether and how we can proceed successfully. This is a very important issue when we look at the development of the Estonian economy and, as a result, the progress of the welfare of all of us. If we look back at the beginning of the 1990s for a moment, we can of course say that we have achieved, for example, the growth of the gross economic product, calculated per capita, with a dizzy acceleration. Income has also increased significantly during the period of independence. Yet we are barely halfway where we would like to go. These accelerations have not come without loss, whether it be human health, social relations, environment or other. Are we ready for new accelerations that need to be even stronger to reach the top, or is smart enough? Or do you need both? I do not know this answer at the moment, but I do know that, for example, in the area of economic development, we have made considerable expenditure on European Union money. To promote, inter alia, innovation and export. Sometimes successful, sometimes not, and that's normal. However, it is not normal that we have not taken from this experience the most important knowledge of how to succeed in the future, how to be more effective. This problem has also been referred to by the previous Minister for Finance, who recently said that investment, that is to say, the distribution of money, is not a substitute for reforms, and only smart investment will lead to the expected growth and jobs. I quote: 'A fair assessment of the domestic economic situation and sometimes painful but necessary decisions should be taken. " Sounds familiar. I believe, because that was the question I highlighted in last year's review. Which country can we afford? I must admit that this question has still not been answered in a year. However, our new acceleration must be driven by smarter and more effective action. In my annual review of this year, I have drawn attention in a number of areas to the problems of lack of knowledge. For example, the restructuring of the education system has kind of begun, but where exactly should it lead to, what should be achieved as a result and by what time it will be unknown. Similarly, what is happening around the development of the oil shale sector and environmental charges can be characterised. While the political era has determined when decisions are to be taken, a number of decisions are not clear, the health, environmental and socio-economic impacts necessary for making decisions and the things affecting energy independence are not clear. The reorganisation of the educational sector and the oil shale sector described in the National Audit Office's review is linked by another problematic common feature that is not visible at first sight, namely the lack of cooperation in government. In the case of the oil shale sector, this means poor cooperation between the Ministry of Finance and the Ministry of the Environment in developing the oil shale-worthy return model, while in the field of education, the lack of cooperation between the Ministry of Education and Research and the Ministry of Finance and the municipalities in order to ensure that the organisation of the school network and the financing model in the field of education ultimately ensure that the teaching profession is well paid and valued and that the teaching staff is sufficient and motivated to educate our subsequent knees. The problems of governance faced by a small open economy and a dispersed country are well summed up by the Sitra survey of the Finnish Innovation Fund "The governments of the future' this year. The study concludes that the current so-called silo governance system is no longer able to cope with the problems facing the country, whether it is social security, health services or education. On the subject of education and teachers, I would like to talk a little bit further. There is no longer any doubt in this Chamber or in Estonia as a whole that education is the key to our future success. I'll start with a positive. Efforts to raise teachers' salaries deserve to be noted. The agreement on substantive orientations in the lifelong learning strategy is to be welcomed. Looking closer to the strategy, we are faced with one basic problem: how to measure and describe the teacher's income? When taking data from official sources, including those available to the public, the teacher must be equated with an educational worker, i.e. a much wider range, which involves more than the teacher facing the class. If we add different data sources for estimated or actually paid wages here, we will get controversial results for some of them. Results that cause confusion and controversy. At the same time, there is a particular problem with the lack of a clear indicator of teacher pay. While recent efforts have been made by the government to increase the pay of teachers, which has also raised the average wage, in a large picture, for example, in comparison with OECD countries, for which the latest data are available, the Estonian teacher is, unfortunately, firmly in the last place with his income. Auditing the field of education also reveals the sad fact that our brighter-headed young people do not choose the teaching profession or career and our faculty ages quietly but firmly. In addition to the confusion in the organisation of the school network, which is reflected in the unequal access to education and the fluctuating quality of education in real life, the question inevitably arises as to why the central government has taken the stand-by position in solving educational problems. Is this a political choice or indecisiveness, or is this indecisiveness a political choice? In any case, such behaviour has its own cost and far-reaching effect. Of course, evolutionary development certainly has its advantages. This will ensure success for stronger and more adaptable specimens according to the laws of nature. Unfortunately, this approach to government is not the most sensible. You don't have to look far for an example. The example that we had to mention this time in our review is, of course, the reform of the municipality. When I spoke earlier about the lack of knowledge in the fields of economy, education and oil shale, the municipality reform is a subject that has been, and indeed has been, the subject of knowledge accumulated over the years, but there is no decision on the active intervention of the state. If we come back to the need to increase Estonia's development at an earlier or even faster pace, regional development that takes into account the peculiarities and strengths of Estonian sites is both a prerequisite for success and a direct contribution to improving the well-being of Estonian people. Without the reform of the local government, the availability of public service will become increasingly unequal for the people of Estonia and the quality of the public service will become increasingly variable. Good listener! I will leave this year's speech without giving any further consideration to the major concerns of the social sector, including the financing of the health care system. You can read about them in the overview of the National Audit Office, you have become more familiar with the issues of labour capacity reform at the debate that took place here last week, as well as at the procedure of the drafts today. You can also read about the problems with the availability of open data and the shortcomings in the audits that ensure the security of information systems. These topics in the field of information and communication technology included in the overview of the National Audit Office are just an introduction. The National Audit Office intends to address developments in e-Estonia more widely in the coming years. Honourable Riigikogu! I have also said before in this Chamber that representatives of the coalition parties of the Riigikogu should particularly be interested in ensuring that the activities of the executive power are more knowledge-based.
