You are the ones who will finally decide on the major reforms, and you can also dictate the pace of these reforms. The National Audit Office wants to continue to be a useful partner for you in this work, also with this review. But about the partnership. I was recently introduced to a comparative study on the impact of performance audits carried out among the highest audit institutions in the Nordic countries. In this study, nearly 120 Estonian officials gave their assessment of the impact of the audit, who have practically been exposed to the audits of the National Audit Office. I bring out two remarkable points. First, the performance audit of the Estonian National Audit Office is considered to be quite a cause of change, especially in comparison with other Nordic countries. Secondly, Estonia is very clearly distinguished by the fact that the members of the Riigikogu do not use the information presented in the audits to initiate changes and make the executive power responsible for the results of their activities. A poor example of this latter aspect is the debate held in the Riigikogu conference hall at the meeting of the State Budget Control Committee this year. The discussion covered the observations and problems of the e-health audit carried out by the National Audit Office and involved the members of the Riigikogu Committee and the heads of Estonian health services and top officials. The Ministry of Social Affairs, as its representative, sent an official to the debate in charge of the functions of Head of Department. The official who was put in this difficult position was good, but no binding promise to change the situation can be given as a good official to the Riigikogu for a minister. This, honourable members of the Riigikogu, is not an acceptable behaviour; you should not accept it and the National Audit Office cannot accept it. I therefore call for further discussions on the audits of the National Audit Office with the participation of the Chancellor or Minister, as the main part of the recommendations of the National Audit Office's audit report is addressed to the Minister. I also assume that the effective cooperation between the Riigikogu and the National Audit Office will lead to more demanding information provided by the government. We must not accept empty reports or hollow explanations, which do not give a true picture of the situation and which have been wasted unnecessarily on time and taxpayers' money. For my part, I assure you that I will continue to contribute to the development of the competence of my auditors and to improving information about the results of the work of the National Audit Office. I continue to consider it very important to establish the capacity of the National Audit Office to audit the areas of internal security and defence. The increase in the audit capacity in this area depends in many respects on the decisions of the Riigikogu Committee on Finance and the Riigikogu. Finally. This year, in addition to the problems, I have also highlighted concrete recommendations more clearly in the review. Consider them a reminder, an inspiration or a solution from which to shape suitable drafts or shape your positions for decision. I can confirm that the National Audit Office will continue to monitor the development of important areas and will highlight risks and problems so that you can do your work as well as possible in this Chamber. Before the forthcoming elections of the Riigikogu, I would remind you once again that all the reports of the National Audit Office are readily and free of charge available on the Riigikogu's website. In order to promote the Estonian government culture and make better decisions, I invite you to get acquainted with them. Do so boldly, openly and consistently, not like the peasants of the Vicar in Odja's orchard, which has been well written by August Gail in his novel 'Ekke Moor'. Thank you for listening.

Something we could say that we could switch coalitions and ministers, not to mention prime ministers, but that the state's ability to solve problems still remains modest. I therefore believe that the need for reforms cannot be limited to individual areas, but must necessarily include reform of state governance. Only by a little overdoing can it be said that the current government, or rather, the government, which operates under the current rule of law and the constitution, cannot decide on any issue that falls between two ministerial chairs. Fortunately, perhaps even if the ministers are from one political party, but when it comes to coalition partners and it is not written in the coalition agreement, forget it. This complaint, or at least the thought, also concerns the Riigikogu, because the Riigikogu's capacity to process inputs is, unfortunately, limited to 101 members of the Riigikogu. The official body of the Riigikogu is so occupied with technical work that there is little potential competence to provide reasonable advice to members of the Riigikogu. That is why I think that we should take seriously, in the coming period of government, as far as possible after the next Riigikogu elections, the reform of the state, which would concern the organisation of the Riigikogu as well as the organisation of governance, as well as the state budgeting, not to mention the reform of the local government, the reform of the European Union's subsidies and tax reform. I hope the voters agree with me. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U782	Please, three minutes!
ParlaMint-EE_2014-11-12_U783	And now for the debate that went to the Committee on Monetary Affairs last week. We learned from the Auditor General's reply that the Ministry of Finance has counted all the figures. Thus, the situation, which should be recognised by both the government and the opposition, is as follows in terms of economic indicators. The purchasing power of Estonian people has increased by 4.1% over the last five years, but it has increased by 8.8% in Latvia and 9.8% in Lithuania. We've progressed slower than our southern neighbours. At the same time, our prices have reached the European Union's average price level much faster, or we could say that our prices have increased, while in Latvia and Lithuania they have fallen compared to the European average. In the last three years, the productivity of Estonia's workforce has not been able to catch up with the European Union average. In five years Estonia's productivity has increased by 4.4%, while in Latvia it has increased by 11.9% and in Lithuania by 12.7%. These are the figures that put the so-called success story in a very clear context and the situation where the party of the Prime Minister says that doing things right and doing things differently would be giving up the story of success. This success story, compared to Latvia and Lithuania, is not so obvious. I don't think it's worth attacking the National Audit Office, just to look at the situation, the government should also look at the situation and think about what can be done differently. In conclusion, I chose point 77 from the report, where the National Audit Office writes that, when comparing Estonia's level of development with the European Union average, the GDP per capita on the basis of purchasing power, productivity and price level, it should be noted that, at least according to these indicators, Estonia did not catch up significantly on the average of the European Union in its development last year. Latvia and Lithuania seem to have succeeded better. Instead of staying down in the competition with our southern neighbours, we should aim for an approach to the Nordic countries. However, it is essential to read the report of the National Audit Office with a broader perspective and learn from it. Thank you for your attention.
